# Empiricism — Complete Learning Session

> **Syllabus, verbatim:** *Empiricism (Locke, Berkeley, Hume) : Theory of Knowledge; Substance and Qualities; Self and God; Scepticism* — UPSC Philosophy Paper I, Section A, item 3.
> **Evidence discipline:** ✅ marks doctrine on which the standard sources converge, ⚠️ marks analytical reconstruction for exam use, and ❓ marks a live scholarly dispute or a contested attribution. Locke is cited by **Book.Chapter.Section** of the *Essay Concerning Human Understanding*, Berkeley by **section number** of the *Principles of Human Knowledge* and by dialogue of the *Three Dialogues*, and Hume by **Book.Part.Section** of the *Treatise of Human Nature* and by **Section** of the *Enquiry concerning Human Understanding*, because those references are identical in every modern edition. Model answers are independent learner practice and are never official UPSC keys.
> **Terminology rule:** the English concept leads and the original Latin term follows immediately — *to be is to be perceived (esse est percipi)*, *blank slate (tabula rasa)*, *evil deceiver*-style vocabulary is not used here, and any Latin tag that is a later label rather than the author's own words is marked as such.

*Concept spine: read it downward. One shared rule about the origin of content, applied with increasing severity to four printed test cases — knowledge, substance and qualities, self and God, and scepticism.*

> 🔑 **Mnemonic — the slide "R → I → S".** **R**epresentative realism → **I**mmaterialism → mitigated **S**cepticism.
> 🔑 **Mnemonic — what each man deletes, "M-S-N".** Berkeley deletes **M**atter; Hume deletes the **S**ubstantial self and objective **N**ecessity.
> 🔑 **Mnemonic — the three verdicts on scepticism, "H-A-M".** Locke is **H**umble, Berkeley is **A**nti-sceptical, Hume is **M**itigated.

### ANSWER-WORTHINESS — WHAT EARNS MARKS AND WHAT DOES NOT

| Classification | Content |
|---|---|
| **CORE MAINS** | Locke's refutation of innate ideas and the sources of ideas; the degrees of knowledge; representative realism; abstract general ideas and nominal versus real essence; primary and secondary qualities; substance as "something I know not what"; personal identity and the circularity objection; Locke's demonstrative God. Berkeley's attack on abstraction; the five reconstructed arguments for immaterialism; *to be is to be perceived (esse est percipi)*; spirits known by notion; God as cause and guarantor of continuity; the contrast with Hegel's absolute idealism; the Moore and Russell reactions. Hume's impressions and ideas and the copy principle; the fork; the dissolution of substance; the bundle self and Kant's reply; the analysis of causation and its two definitions; the problem of induction; the failure of the proofs of God; mitigated scepticism |
| **CORE SUPPORTING** | The three principles of association; the missing shade of blue; constancy and coherence in the belief in body; the prince-and-cobbler case; Reid's brave officer; the *Treatise* Appendix retraction; the billiard balls and the bread; the argument against miracles; the comparative tables; citation and provenance discipline |
| **SUPPORTING** | Locke's corpuscular talk of "insensible corpuscles"; Berkeley's account of illusion, science and other minds; Berkeley on natural law as the settled grammar of a divine language; the Samuel Johnson stone-kicking anecdote as **reception**, never as an argument |
| **OPTIONAL ADVANCED** | Whether Locke's substratum is a humble realist posit or an empty placeholder; whether the Master Argument proves anything beyond a scope confusion; whether Hume is better read as a destructive sceptic or as a naturalist |
| **NOT OWNED HERE** | Descartes', Spinoza's and Leibniz's positive systems (Topic 02); Kant's critical system as a doctrine (Topic 04); Hegel's dialectic as a doctrine (Topic 05); Moore's and Russell's own positive philosophies (Topic 06); Quine's "Two Dogmas of Empiricism" and every question about it (Topic 11); Locke's political philosophy; Berkeley's mathematical controversies; Hume's passions, ethics, history and economics |

⚠️ **Ownership boundary, stated once.** Kant, Hegel, Moore and Russell appear here **only** because three routed questions — 2020 Q2(a), 2024 Q2(a) and 2018 Q3(a) — explicitly demand the response. In each case the empiricist argument must be completed **before** the reply is reported. The three papers that mention Quine's *Two Dogmas of Empiricism* (2021 Q3(c), 2024 Q4(c) and 2025 Q4(b)) share a word with this item and belong entirely to Topic 11.

### ORIGIN AND CHRONOLOGY — THE MINIMUM NEEDED TO AVOID ANACHRONISM

British empiricism is not three separate biographies. It is one rule about the origin of cognitive content, read more strictly at each stage, across roughly sixty years of publication.

```text
 1632-1704  JOHN LOCKE            born Wrington, Somerset; died Oates, Essex
   1689  An Essay Concerning Human Understanding is published
         CAUTION: the title page prints 1690, and the work is almost always
         cited as 1690; both dates are correct in their own sense
   1690  Essay, 1st edition as dated -> Book I  anti-innatism
                                        Book II ideas, qualities, substance
                                        Book III words, abstraction, essence
                                        Book IV  knowledge, degrees, God
   1694  2nd edition ADDS II.xxvii "Of Identity and Diversity"
         CAUTION: personal identity is NOT in the original 1690 text
   1700  4th edition -> the text most modern editions follow
         (standard scholarly text: Nidditch, Clarendon 1975)
                                    |
 1685-1753  GEORGE BERKELEY       Irish; Anglican bishop of Cloyne from 1734
   1710  A Treatise concerning the Principles of Human Knowledge
           Introduction Sec.6-25 -> the attack on abstract general ideas
           Sec.1-4   -> objects of knowledge are ideas; the semantic argument
           Sec.3     -> "to be is to be perceived" (esse est percipi)
           Sec.8     -> the likeness principle: an idea can be like only an idea
           Sec.9-15  -> the parity collapse of primary and secondary qualities
           Sec.22-23 -> the Master Argument
           Sec.27, 89, 140 -> spirits are known by NOTION, not by idea
           Sec.6, 30-33, 48 -> God causes and sustains the order of ideas
   1713  Three Dialogues between Hylas and Philonous -> the same case in dialogue
                                    |
 1711-1776  DAVID HUME            born and died in Edinburgh
   1739-40  A Treatise of Human Nature
           I.i.1   -> impressions and ideas; the missing shade of blue
           I.iii.14 -> the search for the impression of necessary connexion
           I.iv.2  -> constancy, coherence and the belief in body
           I.iv.6  -> "a bundle or collection of different perceptions"
           Appendix -> Hume RETRACTS: he cannot explain what unites the bundle
   1748  An Enquiry concerning Human Understanding
         CAUTION: published in 1748 as Philosophical Essays concerning Human
         Understanding and renamed in 1758; Hume disowned the Treatise as a
         juvenile work, and the Enquiry DROPS personal identity
           II  -> the copy principle | IV -> the problem of induction
           V   -> custom, "the great guide of human life"
           VII -> necessary connexion and the TWO definitions of cause
           X   -> miracles | XII -> mitigated scepticism; "commit it then
                                    to the flames"
   1779  Dialogues concerning Natural Religion (published posthumously)
         -> the critique of the design argument
                                    |
   18th c.  JOSEPH BUTLER and THOMAS REID (Reid 1710-1796) attack Locke on
            personal identity: the circularity charge and the Brave Officer
   REPORTED  Samuel Johnson kicks a stone: "I refute it thus!"
            CAUTION: reception, not an argument. Berkeley's thesis was never
            that the stone is unreal or unfelt.
```

- ✅ The order is causal, not merely chronological. Berkeley reads Locke and quotes his general-triangle passage back at him at *Principles*, Introduction §13; Hume then applies Berkeley's own test to substance, self and causal power.
- ⚠️ **Three cautions that protect marks.** First, never date personal identity to 1690: II.xxvii is a **1694 addition**. Second, never merge the *Treatise* and the *Enquiry* without saying which text you are using, because the *Enquiry* silently drops the bundle theory. Third, the *Dialogues* were published only after Hume's death, so his most damaging attack on natural religion is posthumous.

### SESSION 1 — THE EMPIRICIST RULE, THE FOUR PRINTED TEST CASES, AND WHY THE SEQUENCE MOVES

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Empiricism is the view that the raw materials of thought come from experience, so any idea that cannot be traced back to something experienced is under suspicion — and the three British empiricists disagree about how much of the ordinary world survives that test.

**Technical definition:** British empiricism is the doctrine that every simple cognitive content originates in experience — Locke's outer *sensation* and inner *reflection*, Hume's *impressions* — while the mind's own operations of comparing, combining and abstracting are retained; applied successively to knowledge, substance and qualities, self and God, it yields Locke's representative realism, Berkeley's immaterialism and Hume's mitigated scepticism.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Empiricism is not three biographies sharing a chapter but one rule about the **origin of content** enforced with rising severity: Locke keeps a material world he admits he cannot know, Berkeley keeps the world by deleting the matter behind it, and Hume keeps only perceptions, custom and the beliefs nature makes unavoidable — so the slide from realism to idealism to scepticism is a measure of the rule's consistency, not of philosophical decline.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **origin of content, not denial of reason** — the rule governs materials, not operations
- **sensation and reflection** — experience is two-channelled from the start
- **representative realism → immaterialism → mitigated scepticism**
- **the four printed test cases** — knowledge; substance and qualities; self and God; scepticism
- **the added premise** — each step needs one, and naming it earns the mark
- **natural belief** — what survives when justification does not

**How to use them:** open with the origin-of-content rule rather than with a caricature of "anti-reason"; name the four printed test cases so the examiner sees the syllabus; then show that each move from Locke to Berkeley to Hume requires **one further premise**, and name it.

#### Plain-language visual — one rule, three temperaments

```text
   THE RULE EVERYONE ACCEPTS
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   | If you claim an idea, show me the experience it came from.    |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
        |                      |                        |
   LOCKE says:            BERKELEY says:            HUME says:
   "Fine - and the        "Then stop talking        "Then apply it to
    ideas of primary       about MATTER, which       SUBSTANCE, the SELF
    qualities resemble     no experience ever        and CAUSAL POWER
    the world."            delivers."                too."
        |                      |                        |
   keeps matter           keeps the world,         keeps perceptions,
   but cannot inspect     deletes the matter       loses the substrate,
   it                     behind it                the simple self and
        |                      |                  objective necessity
        v                      v                        v
   REPRESENTATIVE          SUBJECTIVE                MITIGATED
   REALISM                 IDEALISM                  SCEPTICISM
```

**In simple words:** three people are handed the same instruction. The first obeys it about *ideas* but quietly exempts *matter*. The second notices the exemption and removes it. The third notices two more exemptions — the self and causal power — and removes those as well. Nobody changes the rule; they only differ about where they stop applying it. That single sentence, written early, converts a list into an argument.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Origin, not warrant | empiricism is about where content comes from; the rationalist quarrel is about what justifies necessity |
| Experience is two-channelled | outer sensation **and** inner reflection, from Locke onward |
| Operations survive | comparing, combining and abstracting are never denied |
| The slide needs premises | each step adds one; state it |
| Scepticism is the endpoint, not the programme | Hume's conclusion is mitigated, not Pyrrhonian |

> **One-line memory:** empiricism disciplines the **materials** of thought, not the **activity** of thinking.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the four printed test cases and the added premise at each step

```text
   SYLLABUS PRINTS FOUR TEST CASES - EVERY ANSWER SHOULD SHOW THEM
   ==================================================================
   1 THEORY OF KNOWLEDGE      what ideas are, where they come from,
                              and how far they can be trusted
   2 SUBSTANCE AND QUALITIES  what a thing is behind its qualities,
                              and which qualities are really in it
   3 SELF AND GOD             what unites a life, and what can be
                              proved about the divine
   4 SCEPTICISM               how much doubt the rule finally forces
   ==================================================================
                                  |
             THE SAME RULE, APPLIED THREE TIMES, WITH AN ADDED PREMISE
                                  |
   LOCKE ---------------------------------------------------------->
   rule + "ideas of primary qualities RESEMBLE their causes"
        |  ADDED PREMISE: resemblance across the perceptual boundary
        v
   BERKELEY -------------------------------------------------------->
   rule + "an idea can be like nothing but an idea" (Principles Sec.8)
        |  ADDED PREMISE: comparability - a resemblance nobody could
        |  ever check is empty; so matter is not unknown but meaningless
        v
   HUME ------------------------------------------------------------>
   rule + "impressions are separable atoms, so no relation is given
           WITH its terms"
        |  ADDED PREMISE: atomism of impressions; therefore substance,
        |  self and necessary connexion have no source impression
        v
   RESULT: bundles, habit, natural belief and MITIGATED scepticism
   ==================================================================
   WHY KANT MATTERS AT ALL HERE: he accepts the negative result and
   denies the atomism, which is why he is the routed reply in 2020
   and 2025 - and why he is NOT part of the empiricist doctrine.
```

⚠️ **Caption.** The examiner's reward is concentrated in the middle column. A script that reports three positions has described the topic; a script that names the **added premise** at each step has explained it.

#### 1. The rule, stated exactly — and what it does not say

✅ Every **simple** idea traces to experience. Locke's channels are **sensation** (outer sense: colour, sound, warmth, solidity) and **reflection** (inner sense: the mind noticing its own thinking, doubting, willing, believing). Hume's version is the **copy principle**: every simple idea is a faint copy of a corresponding simple impression.

❌ Empiricism is **not** the claim that the mind is inert, that reason is worthless, or that there are no *a priori* truths at all. Hume's own "relations of ideas" are known by thought alone. What is denied is innate **ideas or principles** in Locke's target sense — not native faculties.

#### 2. The four printed test cases, and where each thinker lands

| Test case | Locke | Berkeley | Hume |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Theory of knowledge** | knowledge is the perceived agreement or disagreement of ideas; three degrees | all knowledge is of ideas immediately perceived; no veil | perceptions split into impressions and ideas; the fork sorts all enquiry |
| **Substance and qualities** | primary versus secondary qualities; substratum is "something I know not what" | the distinction collapses; there is no material substance at all | no impression of substance, so it is a fiction of the imagination |
| **Self and God** | self is continuity of consciousness; God is demonstrated causally | self is an active spirit known by notion; God causes and sustains the ideas of sense | self is a bundle of perceptions; every proof of God fails |
| **Scepticism** | rejected; humility, not doubt | explicitly refuted, and blamed on the materialist | embraced, but **mitigated** by nature and custom |

#### 3. Why the sequence moves — the mechanism in four lines

1. Locke's ideas of primary qualities are said to **resemble** qualities in bodies.
2. Berkeley points out that resemblance is a relation between comparable items, and nothing unperceivable is comparable — so the resemblance claim is not merely unverified, it is **unintelligible** (*Principles* §8).
3. Removing matter leaves ideas and the minds that have them, so Berkeley must supply an active cause for the involuntary order of sense: **God**.
4. Hume then asks Berkeley's question about **spirit** and about **causal power**, finds no impression of either, and the sequence terminates in bundles, habit and mitigated scepticism.

⚠️ Each arrow is an argument, not a historical drift. This is why "Locke → Berkeley → Hume" must be presented as a **pressure test** of one premise, and why it is legitimate to say the endpoint reveals the premise to be incomplete — which is exactly the diagnosis Kant makes.

#### 4. What the three men actually share

- ✅ A **genetic** method: explain a concept by exhibiting the experience that produced it.
- ✅ A hostility to *unexperienceable* explanatory posits, whether scholastic forms or Cartesian innate content.
- ✅ A conviction that philosophy should describe the human understanding rather than legislate for it — Locke's "under-labourer" modesty, Berkeley's appeal to the plain man, Hume's science of human nature.
- ❓ What they do **not** share is a verdict on how much of ordinary life survives, which is why calling them "the empiricists" without an internal differentiation loses marks in every comparative question.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** The movement from Locke to Hume is generated by consistency, not by disagreement about the empiricist rule itself.
> **Named evidence.** All three accept that simple content originates in experience; Locke nevertheless asserts at *Essay* II.viii that ideas of primary qualities resemble their causes, Berkeley answers at *Principles* §8 that "an idea can be like nothing but an idea", and Hume's search at *Treatise* I.iii.14 for an impression of necessary connexion finds only a determination of the mind.
> **Analysis.** Each successor removes an exemption his predecessor had quietly granted: Locke exempts matter, Berkeley exempts spirit and causal agency, and Hume exempts nothing, so the terminal scepticism is the rule's own shadow rather than an external attack on it.
> **Qualification.** The sequence is a reconstruction for exam use, and each step needs an additional premise — comparability for Berkeley, the atomism of impressions for Hume — so it demonstrates the rule's severity without proving that the rule is true.

#### Exam link

- Every comparative question in this item — 2018 Q3(a), 2024 Q2(a) — opens more strongly if the added premise is named in the first paragraph.
- **2022 Q4(c)** and **2023 Q2(c)** both begin from the rule stated in §1; the innate-ideas material in Session 2 is the second half of that answer.
- The four printed test cases in §2 are the safest available structure for any unexpected "Empiricism in general" question.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Empiricists deny reason" | They restrict the **origin of content**; Hume's relations of ideas are known by thought alone |
| "Experience means the five senses" | Locke's **reflection** is inner experience of the mind's own operations |
| Presenting the slide as decline | Each step is the previous position taken consistently, which is why Kant treats it as a diagnosis |
| Treating the three as interchangeable "empiricists" | The whole examinable content lies in the differences |
| Importing Quine's *Two Dogmas of Empiricism* | That is Topic 11; sharing the word "empiricism" creates no ownership |

#### Revision notes

- One rule: every simple content traces to experience — sensation and reflection, impressions and ideas.
- Operations are never denied; innate **ideas and principles** are.
- Four printed test cases: knowledge; substance and qualities; self and God; scepticism.
- Added premises: resemblance (Locke) → comparability (Berkeley) → atomism of impressions (Hume).
- Landing points: representative realism → subjective idealism → mitigated scepticism.
- Kant is the routed reply, never part of the doctrine.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — THE EMPIRICIST RULE AND THE PRESSURE SEQUENCE

```text
RULE: EVERY SIMPLE CONTENT TRACES TO EXPERIENCE (SENSATION + REFLECTION)
        |
        v
FOUR PRINTED TEST CASES: KNOWLEDGE | SUBSTANCE+QUALITIES | SELF+GOD | SCEPTICISM
        |
        +--> LOCKE  + resemblance of primary-quality ideas -> REPRESENTATIVE REALISM
        |            cost: the resemblance can never be inspected
        +--> BERKELEY + "an idea can be like only an idea" -> IMMATERIALISM
        |            cost: spirits need a faculty (notion) outside the idea theory
        +--> HUME   + atomism of impressions -> BUNDLES, HABIT, MITIGATED SCEPTICISM
                     cost: nothing explains what unites the bundle
        |
        v
KANT ENTERS ONLY AS THE ROUTED REPLY (2020, 2025): he keeps the negative
result and attacks the atomism
TRAP: not anti-reason | not five senses only | not three biographies
ANSWER LINE: the slide measures the rule's consistency, not philosophy's decline
```

### SESSION 2 — LOCKE I: THE ATTACK ON INNATE IDEAS, THE BLANK PAGE, AND THE CONSTRUCTION OF IDEAS

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Locke argues that we are not born knowing anything; the mind starts as unwritten paper, experience writes on it through the outer senses and through our awareness of our own mental operations, and everything complex is then built by the mind out of those simple deliveries.

**Technical definition:** Locke's genetic epistemology denies innate ideas and innate practical or speculative principles, derives all ideas from **sensation** and **reflection**, distinguishes passively received **simple ideas** from actively constructed **complex ideas** — sorted as **modes, substances and relations** — and thereby makes the origin of an idea the test of its legitimacy.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Locke's refutation of innate ideas is not an appeal to nursery observation but a demand for redundancy: since every idea alleged to be innate can be shown to arrive through sensation or reflection, and since "to be in the mind" means for Locke "to be perceived by the mind", the innatist hypothesis explains nothing that experience has not already explained and posits something the theory of ideas cannot contain.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **universal assent refuted** — children and the cognitively impaired lack the alleged content
- **no unconscious ideas** — an unperceived thought is a contradiction in Locke's sense
- **the redundancy or genetic argument** — experience suffices, so innateness is idle
- **white paper, void of all characters** — Locke's own words at II.i.2
- **blank slate (tabula rasa)** — a **later label**, not Locke's phrase
- **sensation and reflection** — the two fountains of knowledge
- **simple → complex: modes, substances, relations**

**How to use them:** give the three grounds in order, then the decisive premise about what "in the mind" means, then the concession that Descartes' *dispositional* innatism survives it — that concession is what separates a first-class answer from a textbook one.

#### Plain-language visual — three ways to attack an inborn idea

```text
   CLAIM UNDER ATTACK: "Some ideas are stamped on the mind at birth."

   ATTACK 1  UNIVERSAL ASSENT           ATTACK 2  NO HIDDEN IDEAS
   +---------------------------+        +----------------------------+
   | If it were inborn,        |        | "There is an idea in your  |
   | EVERYONE would have it.   |        |  mind you have never       |
   | Children and the          |        |  thought" is, for Locke,   |
   | cognitively impaired      |        |  a contradiction: to be in  |
   | do not.                   |        |  the mind IS to be         |
   +---------------------------+        |  perceived by the mind.    |
                                        +----------------------------+
                       \                    /
                        \                  /
                    ATTACK 3  REDUNDANCY
                    +--------------------------------+
                    | Every "innate" idea can be     |
                    | traced to sensation or         |
                    | reflection. If experience      |
                    | already explains it, the       |
                    | inborn hypothesis is idle.     |
                    +--------------------------------+
```

**In simple words:** Locke never claims that babies are stupid. He claims that if you say an idea was inside all along but nobody noticed, you have described nothing that experience cannot describe better — and you have used the phrase "in the mind" in a way that, on his account of ideas, has no meaning. Attack 3 is the one that does the philosophical work; attacks 1 and 2 clear the ground for it.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Target | innate **ideas and principles**, not native faculties |
| "White paper" | Locke's own words; *tabula rasa* is the tradition's label |
| Sensation | ideas from external objects acting on the senses |
| Reflection | ideas from noticing the mind's own operations |
| Simple ideas | passively received; the mind cannot invent a new one |
| Complex ideas | actively made: modes, substances, relations |

> **One-line memory:** the mind supplies the **workmanship**; experience supplies the **stock**.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the pipeline from experience to knowledge, with the limit marked

```text
   EXPERIENCE, IN TWO CHANNELS
   =====================================================================
   SENSATION (outer)                    REFLECTION (inner)
   colour, sound, taste, smell,         thinking, doubting, believing,
   warmth, solidity, extension,         willing, reasoning, knowing
   figure, motion, number               - the mind observing ITSELF
   =====================================================================
                  \                    /
                   v                  v
              SIMPLE IDEAS  (PASSIVELY RECEIVED)
              the mind can neither make nor destroy one
                          |
              ACTIVE OPERATIONS: combine | compare | abstract
                          |
                          v
              COMPLEX IDEAS - three kinds
   +-----------------+------------------------+---------------------+
   | MODES           | SUBSTANCES             | RELATIONS           |
   | dependent com-  | supposed independent   | produced by         |
   | binations, e.g. | bearers, represented   | comparing one       |
   | triangle,       | through clusters of    | thing with another: |
   | gratitude,      | co-occurring qualities | cause, identity,    |
   | number          | (gold, man, lead)      | moral relation      |
   +-----------------+------------------------+---------------------+
                          |
                          v
   WHAT LOCKE HAS BOUGHT: a theory in which the ORIGIN of an idea
   licenses its use - and a bill he must pay in Book IV, because
   knowledge is now confined to relations among IDEAS.
   =====================================================================
   THE LIMIT, STATED HONESTLY: the genetic argument shows innateness is
   UNNECESSARY, not that it is IMPOSSIBLE. Descartes' dispositional
   innatism - a capacity elicited by experience - slips past all three
   attacks, and 2023 Q2(c) is decided on exactly this point.
```

⚠️ **Caption.** Read the diagram as an account with a debt. Sessions 3 and 4 collect the debt: if knowledge is the perception of agreement among ideas, the external world must be reached by an inference the theory has made difficult.

#### 1. The three grounds of refutation, in Locke's order

| Ground | Locke's argument | The target |
|---|---|---|
| **Universal assent fails** | If certain ideas or principles were innate, every human being — including children and "idiots" — would possess them; manifestly they do not | The standard innatist appeal to universal agreement |
| **No unconscious ideas** | A thought the mind does not think is self-contradictory, because for Locke "to be in the mind" is "to be perceived by the mind" | The retreat to ideas present but unnoticed |
| **A genetic account is available** | Every idea claimed as innate — God, identity, non-contradiction — can be shown to arise from sensation or reflection; if experience suffices, innateness is idle | All alleged innate content, speculative and practical |

✅ Ground 2 is the decisive one. Grounds 1 and 3 are empirical and can be conceded by a determined innatist; ground 2 is a **stipulation about what "in the mind" means**, and it is that stipulation, not the nursery evidence, that closes the escape route.

#### 2. The blank page — the exact words and the exact provenance

- ✅ Locke writes that the mind is "**white paper, void of all characters**" (*Essay* II.i.2).
- ⚠️ **Provenance mark worth free marks:** *tabula rasa* is **not Locke's phrase**. It is Scholastic and Aristotelian vocabulary applied to him by the tradition. Write: "the mind as 'white paper, void of all characters' — the doctrine traditionally labelled *tabula rasa*."
- ❌ The blank-page thesis does **not** say the mind is passive. It denies innate **content**; it leaves the faculties of perceiving, remembering, comparing, combining and abstracting entirely intact.

#### 3. The two fountains, and why reflection matters

- **Sensation** delivers ideas from external objects impinging on the senses.
- **Reflection** delivers ideas of the mind's own operations — thinking, doubting, willing, believing.
- ⚠️ Reflection is the single most under-used word in weak answers. It is what allows Locke to be an empiricist about **logic, mathematics and the self** without appealing to innate content, and it is what Hume later exploits when he locates the impression of necessary connexion **internally**.

#### 4. Simple and complex ideas — the workmanship of the understanding

- ✅ **Simple ideas** are passively received and unanalysable; the mind cannot invent a wholly new simple sensory content, which is why a person born blind has no idea of scarlet.
- ✅ **Complex ideas** are actively formed by combining, comparing and abstracting:
  - **modes** — dependent combinations such as triangle, gratitude or number;
  - **substances** — supposed independently existing bearers, represented through clusters of qualities;
  - **relations** — ideas produced by comparing one thing with another, such as cause, identity or moral relation.
- ⚠️ The classification is not decoration. "Substance" appearing as a *complex idea* is already the admission that Session 5 exploits: what we have is a cluster plus a supposition, never an inspected substratum.

#### 5. The reply Locke must face — and the honest verdict

- ❓ **Descartes' dispositional reply.** Innate ideas were never claimed to be consciously present from birth; they are **capacities** of the intellect, elicited on an appropriate occasion, in the way a family disease is innate before it appears.
- ⚠️ **Adjudication.** Against a **naive** innatism of consciously possessed inborn propositions, Locke wins outright. Against the **dispositional** innatism Descartes actually held, ground 1 misses and ground 3 is inconclusive, so everything turns on ground 2 — and ground 2 is a stipulation, not a discovery.
- ✅ **The line that earns the mark:** Leibniz's later formula — nothing is in the intellect that was not first in the senses, **except the intellect itself** — shows that innatism survives Locke as a thesis about *structure*, which is precisely the form Kant will vindicate.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Locke's refutation of innate ideas succeeds against the doctrine as he states it and leaves the dispositional version standing.
> **Named evidence.** *Essay* Book I mounts three attacks — the failure of universal assent, the impossibility of an idea in the mind that the mind does not perceive, and the availability of a genetic account through sensation and reflection — while II.i.2 replaces innate content with "white paper, void of all characters".
> **Analysis.** The empirical grounds can be absorbed by an innatist who claims only a capacity, so the weight falls on Locke's premise that to be in the mind is to be perceived by it; that premise is a decision about the meaning of "idea" rather than an observation, and once it is questioned the refutation loses its grip.
> **Qualification.** The dispute is therefore not empirical but conceptual, and Leibniz's rejoinder that the intellect itself is what the senses never supplied shows the surviving form of innatism to be structural — the form Kant later develops, and the reason 2023 Q2(c) cannot be answered by listing Locke's points alone.

#### Exam link

- **2022 Q4(c), 15 marks** — "Why and how does John Locke refute the innate ideas? Elucidate the nature and source of knowledge in Locke's epistemology." §§1–4 are the whole answer, with Session 3 supplying the degrees of knowledge.
- **2023 Q2(c), 15 marks** — routed to Rationalism, but the Locke half is §1 and §5; the dispositional reply is compulsory there.
- Any question on empiricist method opens from §2 and §3.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| Quoting "*tabula rasa*" as Locke's words | His words are "white paper, void of all characters"; the Latin tag is the tradition's |
| "Blank slate means the mind is passive" | Only **simple** ideas are passive; complex ideas are the workmanship of the understanding |
| Giving only the children-and-idiots argument | That is the weakest ground; the decisive one is that nothing can be in the mind unperceived |
| Omitting reflection | Without it Locke cannot explain ideas of thinking, willing or the self, and the answer looks like crude sensationalism |
| Presenting the refutation as complete | Dispositional innatism survives; say so and grade the verdict |

#### Revision notes

- Three grounds: universal assent fails · no unconscious ideas · genetic account available.
- Decisive premise: "in the mind" = "perceived by the mind" — a stipulation.
- II.i.2: "white paper, void of all characters"; *tabula rasa* is a later label.
- Two fountains: sensation (outer) and reflection (inner).
- Simple ideas passive; complex ideas = modes, substances, relations.
- Verdict: naive innatism destroyed; dispositional innatism survives; Leibniz's "except the intellect itself" states the survivor.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — LOCKE I: INNATE IDEAS AND THE SOURCES OF IDEAS

```text
INNATIST CLAIM: SOME IDEAS ARE STAMPED ON THE MIND AT BIRTH
        |
        +--> GROUND 1 universal assent fails (children, the impaired)
        +--> GROUND 2 no idea can be in the mind unperceived  <-- DECISIVE
        +--> GROUND 3 experience already explains every candidate
        |
        v
REPLACEMENT: "WHITE PAPER, VOID OF ALL CHARACTERS" (Essay II.i.2)
   label caution: tabula rasa is the tradition's phrase, not Locke's
        |
        v
TWO FOUNTAINS: SENSATION (outer) + REFLECTION (inner operations)
        |
        v
SIMPLE IDEAS (passive) --combine/compare/abstract--> COMPLEX IDEAS
        MODES | SUBSTANCES | RELATIONS
        |
        v
RESIDUAL: dispositional innatism survives; Leibniz - "except the intellect
itself"; Kant later vindicates the structural form
TRAP: tabula rasa as a quotation | passivity | omitting reflection
ANSWER LINE: Locke shows innateness is unnecessary, not impossible
```

### SESSION 3 — LOCKE II: KNOWLEDGE AS PERCEIVED AGREEMENT, THE THREE DEGREES, PROBABILITY AND THE VEIL OF PERCEPTION

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** For Locke, to know something is to see that two of your own ideas do or do not fit together; that seeing comes in three strengths, and everything below the weakest strength is not knowledge but reasonable belief.

**Technical definition:** Locke defines knowledge as the perception of the agreement or disagreement of ideas, grades it as **intuitive**, **demonstrative** and **sensitive**, assigns everything beyond these grades to **probability** and judgement, and combines this with **representative realism** — the doctrine that we perceive ideas which represent external objects, generating the epistemic gap his successors exploit.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Locke's theory of knowledge is generous in its account of where ideas come from and severe in its account of what may be called knowledge: because knowing is the perception of agreement **between ideas**, the existence of the external world can be reached only by the weakest of his three degrees, and that structural asymmetry — rich origins, thin certification — is what makes representative realism unstable enough for Berkeley to attack.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **perception of the agreement or disagreement of ideas**
- **intuitive, demonstrative, sensitive** — the three degrees, in that order of certainty
- **probability and judgement** — the vast domain below knowledge
- **representative realism** — ideas represent; objects are inferred
- **the veil of perception** — the gap the theory creates
- **sensitive knowledge** — Locke's own weakest degree, and the exam pressure point

**How to use them:** define knowledge first, then the three degrees with one example each, then show that the external world sits at the bottom of the ladder — and finish by naming the veil objection rather than waiting for the examiner to name it.

#### Plain-language visual — a ladder with a shaky bottom rung

```text
   HOW SURE CAN LOCKE BE?
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   | INTUITIVE     "white is not black"                           |  most
   |               the mind sees the agreement IMMEDIATELY        |  certain
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   | DEMONSTRATIVE  proofs in mathematics; the existence of God   |
   |                agreement seen through INTERVENING ideas      |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   | SENSITIVE      "there is a sheet of paper before me now"     |  least
   |                the existence of PARTICULAR external things   |  certain
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
                   ------ below this line is not knowledge ------
   | PROBABILITY    testimony, analogy, everyday expectation,      |
   |                the whole of natural history and much science  |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
```

**In simple words:** Locke is not a sceptic, but he is a minimalist about the word "knowledge". Most of what we confidently believe — that the sun will rise, that this medicine works, that a friend is trustworthy — is not knowledge on his definition at all; it is well-grounded probability. Saying this early shows the examiner that you understand the **scale** of his restriction, which is the point Hume will later exploit without mercy.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Knowledge | perceived agreement or disagreement of ideas |
| Intuitive | immediate, no middle term |
| Demonstrative | mediated by intervening ideas; a chain of intuitions |
| Sensitive | that particular things exist; the weakest degree |
| Probability | judgement, not knowledge; where most of life happens |
| Representative realism | we perceive ideas that represent objects |

> **One-line memory:** rich sources, **narrow** certification — and the world sits on the bottom rung.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the causal chain, the representation claim, and the gap it opens

```text
   THE CHAIN LOCKE ACCEPTS
   =====================================================================
   EXTERNAL BODY ---acts on---> SENSE ORGAN ---produces---> IDEA IN MIND
   (has primary qualities;      (mechanically              (the only thing
    real essence unknown)        affected)                  ever inspected)
   =====================================================================
                                                               |
                                            KNOWLEDGE OPERATES ONLY HERE:
                                            agreement AMONG IDEAS
                                                               |
   THE REPRESENTATION CLAIM: ideas of PRIMARY qualities RESEMBLE
   their causes; ideas of SECONDARY qualities do not resemble anything.
                                                               |
                                                               v
   THE VEIL OBJECTION - state it yourself before the examiner does:
   +-----------------------------------------------------------------+
   | To check that an idea resembles its cause I must compare the two.|
   | But I can only ever inspect the IDEA. So the resemblance is a    |
   | claim I am, in principle, unable to test.                        |
   +-----------------------------------------------------------------+
        |                                   |
   LOCKE'S REPLIES                      WHAT SURVIVES
   1 the involuntariness and            a CAUSAL and STRUCTURAL claim
     coherence of sense                 (this idea was produced by that
   2 the agreement of different         body) is defensible; the further
     senses about the same body         QUALITATIVE likeness claim is the
   3 God is no deceiver                 exposed one - and it is precisely
   4 sensitive knowledge is             what Berkeley attacks at
     "as certain as our frame           Principles Sec.8
     can attain"
   =====================================================================
```

⚠️ **Caption.** Do not present the veil as a knock-down refutation. Present it as the **cost** of an otherwise powerful theory, list Locke's four replies, and then say exactly which part of the representation claim survives. That is the difference between reporting a criticism and adjudicating one.

#### 1. The definition, and why it constrains everything after it

✅ **Knowledge is the perception of the agreement or disagreement of ideas.** Everything Locke can subsequently say about the world must be expressed as a relation among ideas — which is why the existence of things outside the mind is the hardest thing for his system to certify, and why he must introduce a third, weaker degree specially for it.

#### 2. The three degrees, with their examples

| Degree | Mechanism | Example | Certainty |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Intuitive** | the agreement is seen immediately, with no intervening idea | "white is not black"; "a circle is not a triangle"; my own existence | highest |
| **Demonstrative** | the agreement is seen through a chain of intervening ideas, each link intuitive | mathematical proof; the existence of God | high, but liable to error in long chains |
| **Sensitive** | the existence of particular things now affecting the senses | "there is a sheet of paper before me" | lowest of the three, "though still reliable" |

✅ Naming **sensitive knowledge** as Locke's own term, and admitting it is his weakest degree, is what allows you to explain the internal pressure on the system without misrepresenting him as a sceptic.

#### 3. Probability — the domain Locke does not call knowledge

- ⚠️ Beyond the three degrees lies **judgement**, governed by probability: the conformity of a claim with our own experience, and the testimony of others weighed by number, integrity, skill and consistency.
- ✅ This is where most of natural philosophy, all history and all everyday reasoning live. Locke's epistemic humility is not doubt; it is a careful refusal to inflate belief into knowledge.
- ❓ The unresolved tension: sensitive knowledge and high probability shade into one another, and Locke never draws the boundary sharply.

#### 4. Representative realism, and the four defences

- ✅ We do not perceive objects directly; we perceive **ideas** that represent external objects.
- ⚠️ The gap is real, and Locke does not ignore it. His defences are: sense ideas are **involuntary**; different senses **agree** about the same body; pleasure and pain accompany real bodies in ways imagination cannot reproduce; and God would not construct us to be systematically deceived in what matters for the conduct of life.
- ✅ **What survives the veil objection:** a **causal** and **structural** representation claim — that this idea was produced by that body, and that measurable structure corresponds. What is exposed is the further **qualitative resemblance** claim, and that is the exact target of *Principles* §8.

#### 5. Why Locke is not a sceptic

- ✅ He denies that we can know **real essences** or the intrinsic nature of substance; he does not deny that we know enough for "the conveniences of life and the information of virtue".
- ⚠️ The famous image is the plumb line: our candle burns bright enough for our purposes, and complaining that it is not the sun is a misuse of the faculties we have.
- ❌ Never write that Locke "leads to scepticism" as though he endorsed it. Write that his structure **makes a sceptical challenge available**, and that Berkeley claims to remove the challenge by removing the veil.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Locke's epistemology is generous about the origin of ideas and severe about the certification of knowledge, and that asymmetry rather than any sceptical intention is what destabilises representative realism.
> **Named evidence.** Knowledge is defined as the perception of the agreement or disagreement of ideas and graded as intuitive, demonstrative and sensitive, with the existence of particular external things assigned to the weakest degree, while everything else — testimony, analogy, natural history — is relegated to probability and judgement.
> **Analysis.** Because all certification is internal to ideas, the claim that ideas of primary qualities resemble their causes cannot be checked by the only procedure the system allows, so Berkeley's later objection is not an external attack but a demand that Locke apply his own standard to his own thesis.
> **Qualification.** The damage is nevertheless limited: the involuntariness and inter-sensory agreement of sense support a causal and structural representation claim, and only the qualitative resemblance thesis is genuinely exposed — which is why Locke should be read as an epistemically humble realist rather than as an unwilling sceptic.

#### Exam link

- **2022 Q4(c), 15 marks** — the second limb, "the nature and source of knowledge in Locke's epistemology", is answered from §§1–3.
- **2024 Q2(a), 20 marks** — the veil and the resemblance thesis in §4 are what Berkeley's rejection of primary qualities exploits.
- **2021 Q1(c), 10 marks** — Berkeley's "immediately perceived" is defined against Locke's mediated perception, so §4 is the contrast this answer needs.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Locke is a sceptic" | He is a moderate realist with a narrow definition of knowledge |
| Listing the three degrees without examples | The examples are what show you understand the mechanism |
| Ignoring probability | Most of Locke's own epistemic life happens below the knowledge line |
| Treating the veil as Locke's own doctrine | "Veil of perception" is the critics' image, not his phrase |
| Saying representation is refuted outright | Causal and structural representation survives; qualitative resemblance does not |

#### Revision notes

- Knowledge = perceived agreement or disagreement of ideas.
- Degrees: intuitive (immediate) → demonstrative (chained) → sensitive (particular existents).
- Below knowledge: probability and judgement — testimony, analogy, natural history.
- Representative realism: ideas represent; objects are inferred.
- Four defences: involuntariness · inter-sensory agreement · pleasure and pain · divine non-deception.
- Survives: causal and structural representation. Exposed: qualitative resemblance.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — LOCKE II: KNOWLEDGE, DEGREES AND THE VEIL

```text
KNOWLEDGE = PERCEIVED AGREEMENT OR DISAGREEMENT OF IDEAS
        |
        +--> INTUITIVE     immediate: "white is not black"
        +--> DEMONSTRATIVE  chained: mathematics; the existence of God
        +--> SENSITIVE      particular external things - WEAKEST DEGREE
        |
        v
BELOW THE LINE: PROBABILITY AND JUDGEMENT (testimony, analogy, science)
        |
        v
REPRESENTATIVE REALISM: body -> sense organ -> IDEA (the only inspectable item)
   claim: primary-quality ideas RESEMBLE; secondary-quality ideas do NOT
        |
        +--> VEIL OBJECTION: the comparison can never be made
        +--> LOCKE'S REPLIES: involuntariness | inter-sensory agreement |
             pleasure and pain | God is no deceiver
        |
        v
SURVIVES: causal + structural representation | EXPOSED: qualitative likeness
TRAP: Locke as sceptic | degrees without examples | veil as his own phrase
ANSWER LINE: generous about origins, severe about certification
```

### SESSION 4 — LOCKE III: ABSTRACT GENERAL IDEAS, NOMINAL AND REAL ESSENCE, AND THE GENERAL TRIANGLE

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Everything that exists is a particular thing, yet almost every word we use is general; Locke explains the gap by saying the mind strips away what makes ideas particular and keeps a stripped-down idea that stands for a whole class — and the class-name then means that stripped-down idea, not the hidden inner nature of the things.

**Technical definition:** Locke's doctrine of **abstract general ideas** holds that words become general by signifying general ideas, and ideas become general by **abstraction** — separation from the circumstances of time, place and all concomitant ideas — producing a "general representative" that is the **nominal essence** annexed to a sortal name, as distinct from the unknown **real essence** or internal constitution of the thing.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Locke's doctrine of abstraction is the hinge of the whole empiricist quarrel about language: because he holds that everything existing is particular, that words are general only by signifying general ideas, and that classification is therefore "the workmanship of the understanding", the general idea of a triangle must be "all and none" of the determinate triangles at once — a concession he makes himself at *Essay* IV.vii.9, and the concession on which Berkeley builds his entire immaterialism.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **everything that exists is particular** (*Essay* III.iii.1)
- **abstraction** — separation from "circumstances of time and place"
- **general representative** — the abstract idea's job
- **nominal essence** versus **real essence**
- **conceptualism, not Platonic realism** — universals are creatures of the understanding
- **"something imperfect, that cannot exist"** — Locke's own admission

**How to use them:** state the eight-step argument, produce the triangle passage, and then — before any objection is raised — distinguish an idea with a feature **left undetermined** from an idea with **contradictory features assigned**. That distinction is Locke's best defence and it decides the 2025 answer.

#### Plain-language visual — how one idea can stand for many things

```text
   THE PROBLEM
   things that exist:   this dog | that dog | the other dog  (all particular)
   words we use:        "dog"                                (general)
                          ^
                          |
   LOCKE'S SOLUTION: SUBTRACTION
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | Take the idea of THIS dog.  Remove: the time you saw it, the   |
   | place, its colour, its size, its bark, its owner...            |
   | What is left is a stripped idea that can stand for ANY dog.    |
   | That stripped idea IS the meaning of the word "dog".           |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                          |
   BERKELEY'S REPLY: SELECTION, NOT SUBTRACTION
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | Keep the idea of THIS dog, complete and determinate. Simply    |
   | ATTEND to what it shares with others, and let it STAND FOR     |
   | all of them. The geometer draws this black inch-long line and  |
   | proves a theorem about all lines.                              |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
```

**In simple words:** the whole dispute is whether generality lives in a **special stripped idea** or in the **use** to which an ordinary idea is put. Locke says the idea is special; Berkeley says the use is special. Once you can say that sentence, you can answer the 2025 question in your first line.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Particularity | everything that exists is particular |
| Abstraction | strip time, place and concomitants |
| General representative | the stripped idea standing for the sort |
| Nominal essence | the abstract idea annexed to the general name |
| Real essence | the internal constitution producing observable properties |
| Conceptualism | universals are inventions of the understanding, not things |

> **One-line memory:** we classify by **nominal** essence and are ignorant of **real** essence — so sorting is our workmanship, not nature's dictation.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the eight-step argument, the triangle, and the two essences

```text
   LOCKE'S ARGUMENT, NUMBERED  (Essay III.iii and IV.vii.9)
   =====================================================================
   1 Everything that exists is PARTICULAR                    III.iii.1
   2 A separate name for every particular is impossible -
     memory could not hold them and communication would fail III.iii.2-4
   3 Therefore language requires GENERAL TERMS
   4 Words are general only by being signs of GENERAL IDEAS  III.iii.6
   5 Ideas become general when the mind SEPARATES them from
     circumstances of time, place and every concomitant idea III.iii.6-9
   6 What remains serves as a "GENERAL REPRESENTATIVE" of all
     particulars of the same sort
   7 The abstract idea annexed to a sortal name IS the
     NOMINAL ESSENCE by which we classify                    III.iii.15
   8 "General and universal belong not to the real existence
     of things; they are the inventions and creatures of the
     understanding"                                          III.iii.11
   =====================================================================
                                 |
   THE CANONICAL EXAMPLE - AND THE ADMISSION            Essay IV.vii.9
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | The general idea of a triangle "must be neither oblique nor        |
   | rectangle, neither equilateral, equicrural, nor scalenon; but      |
   | all and none of these at once."                                    |
   | Locke concedes it is "something IMPERFECT, THAT CANNOT EXIST",     |
   | and that framing it "requires some pains and skill."               |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
                                 |
   TWO ESSENCES - KEEP THEM APART
   +--------------------------------+--------------------------------+
   | NOMINAL ESSENCE                | REAL ESSENCE                   |
   | the abstract idea annexed to   | the internal constitution      |
   | the name: "gold" = yellow,     | (Locke's "insensible           |
   | heavy, malleable, soluble in   | corpuscles") from which the    |
   | aqua regia                     | observable properties flow     |
   | KNOWN - we made it             | UNKNOWN - we posit it          |
   | fixes CLASSIFICATION           | fixes NOTHING we can use       |
   +--------------------------------+--------------------------------+
                                 |
   FOUR PRESUPPOSITIONS THE ARGUMENT NEEDS
   P1 meaning is IDEATIONAL - a word signifies by standing for an idea
   P2 ideas are IMAGISTIC and particular in origin
   P3 mental SUBTRACTION leaves something still contentful
   P4 ONE IDEA PER GENERAL WORD - generality lives in the idea, not the use
   =====================================================================
```

⚠️ **Caption.** Presuppositions P1 and P4 are the load-bearing ones. Berkeley's attack in Session 6 destroys P2 and P3; Wittgenstein's later attack destroys P1 and P4, which is why the deepest verdict on this dispute is that the casualty is the **ideational theory of meaning**, not abstraction as such.

#### 1. Why Locke needs the doctrine at all

The doctrine is not an optional flourish. Without it, Locke has no account of how a mind furnished only with particular ideas can use general words, and therefore no account of classification, of definition, or of the nominal essences on which the whole of Book III depends. Berkeley understood this exactly, which is why he attacks abstraction **first**, in the Introduction, before saying a word about matter.

#### 2. The two essences, applied

- ✅ **Real essence** = the internal constitution of a thing — for Locke, the arrangement of its "insensible corpuscles" — that gives rise to all its observable properties. We usually do not know it.
- ✅ **Nominal essence** = the abstract idea associated with a general name. Classification proceeds by nominal essence.
- ⚠️ Consequence worth stating: because we sort by nominal essence, **species boundaries are conventional in their sharpness though not arbitrary in their materials**. Locke is agnostic about real essences without denying that things have them; that is epistemic humility, not anti-realism.

#### 3. Locke as a conceptualist

- ✅ Universals are neither Platonic Forms subsisting apart nor mere noises: they are ideas made by the understanding for its own use, and they "concern only signs" (III.iii.11).
- ⚠️ This places Locke between Plato and the strict nominalist. Naming the position — **conceptualism** — is a one-word gain in precision that most scripts never make.

#### 4. The concession that decides the dispute

- ✅ Locke himself says the abstract general idea of a triangle is "**something imperfect, that cannot exist**". Berkeley quotes the passage **verbatim** at *Principles*, Introduction §13.
- ⚠️ **Locke's best available reply** — the distinction that saves him: an abstract idea is **partial**, not **contradictory**. It is the idea of three-sidedness with the specific angles **left undetermined**, not with contradictory angles **assigned**. Determinables need not be determinate.
- ❓ **The price of that reply:** it requires giving up the imagistic model of ideas that Locke elsewhere retains. Modern commentators generally accept the determinable reading, but it turns the abstract idea into a non-imagistic act of the understanding — and then it is no longer an "idea" in Locke's own official sense.

#### 5. The verdict to carry into Session 6

> ⚠️ **Executable verdict:** Berkeley wins against the **imagistic** Locke and loses against the **determinable** Locke. The lasting damage is not to abstraction but to the ideational theory of meaning that made abstraction necessary — which is why the argument's true heir is not Hume but Wittgenstein's family-resemblance treatment of generality.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Locke's doctrine of abstract general ideas is an answer to a genuine problem about generality, and it is vulnerable only on a particular reading of what an idea is.
> **Named evidence.** *Essay* III.iii.1–15 argues that all existents are particular, that words are general by signifying general ideas, that ideas become general by separation from circumstances of time and place, and that the resulting nominal essence is what classification uses, while IV.vii.9 concedes that the general triangle is "all and none" of the determinate triangles and is "something imperfect, that cannot exist".
> **Analysis.** The concession is fatal only if ideas must be images, for an image cannot be indeterminate; if the abstract idea is instead a determinable with its specific angles left unassigned, the alleged contradiction dissolves and Berkeley's *Principles* Introduction §13 objection misses its target.
> **Qualification.** The rescue is not free, since a non-imagistic determinable is not an idea in Locke's own official sense, so the dispute ends by exposing the ideational theory of meaning itself — the presupposition both men share and neither examines.

#### Exam link

- **2025 Q4(c), 15 marks** — "Explain Berkeley's doctrine of nominalism and his refutation of Abstract ideas." The refutation cannot be explained without the doctrine refuted; this session is the first half of that answer.
- **2022 Q4(c), 15 marks** — nominal essence is part of "the nature and source of knowledge in Locke's epistemology".
- Any question on Locke's account of language or classification is answered from §§1–3.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| Confusing nominal with real essence | Nominal is the idea we made and use; real is the internal constitution we do not know |
| Calling Locke a Platonic realist about universals | He is a **conceptualist**: universals are creatures of the understanding |
| Reporting the triangle as Berkeley's example | It is **Locke's own**, quoted back at him at *Principles* Intro §13 |
| Treating abstraction as obviously absurd | The determinable reading is a serious defence; grade it |
| Forgetting that abstraction is the base of immaterialism | Berkeley's "matter" is an abstract general idea; kill abstraction and matter goes with it |

#### Revision notes

- All existents particular; general words need general ideas; ideas made general by abstraction.
- Abstraction = separation from time, place and concomitants → **general representative**.
- Nominal essence = abstract idea annexed to the name; real essence = unknown internal constitution.
- Locke is a **conceptualist**; universals "concern only signs" (III.iii.11).
- IV.vii.9: the general triangle is "all and none" and "imperfect, that cannot exist".
- Best reply: **partial, not contradictory** — determinable versus determinate.
- Four presuppositions: ideational meaning · imagistic ideas · possible subtraction · one idea per word.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — LOCKE III: ABSTRACTION AND ESSENCE

```text
ALL EXISTENTS ARE PARTICULAR (III.iii.1) BUT WORDS ARE GENERAL
        |
        v
WORDS ARE GENERAL BY SIGNIFYING GENERAL IDEAS (III.iii.6)
        |
        v
ABSTRACTION: strip time, place, concomitants -> GENERAL REPRESENTATIVE
        |
        +--> NOMINAL ESSENCE: the abstract idea annexed to the name (known)
        +--> REAL ESSENCE: internal constitution producing properties (unknown)
        |
        v
CONCEPTUALISM: universals are "inventions and creatures of the understanding"
        |
        v
THE TRIANGLE (IV.vii.9): "all and none"; "imperfect, that cannot exist"
        |
        +--> BERKELEY quotes it verbatim at Principles Intro Sec.13
        +--> LOCKE'S BEST REPLY: partial, NOT contradictory (determinable)
        +--> PRICE: the abstract idea stops being imagistic
        |
        v
VERDICT: Berkeley beats imagistic Locke, loses to determinable Locke; the real
casualty is the IDEATIONAL THEORY OF MEANING
TRAP: nominal vs real | Platonic misreading | triangle attributed to Berkeley
ANSWER LINE: classification is our workmanship, not nature's dictation
```

### SESSION 5 — LOCKE IV: PRIMARY AND SECONDARY QUALITIES AND THE UNKNOWN SUBSTRATUM

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Locke separates the features a body really has whatever anyone thinks — its solidity, size, shape, motion and number — from the features that are only its power to make us see colours, hear sounds or feel warmth; and behind both he supposes a support he admits he cannot describe.

**Technical definition:** Locke's doctrine of qualities distinguishes **primary qualities**, which are inseparable from body and whose ideas **resemble** them, from **secondary qualities**, which are powers grounded in primary-quality configurations to produce sensations in us and whose ideas resemble nothing in the object; both are borne by a supposed **substratum** of which we have "no positive idea" — the famous "something, I know not what" of *Essay* II.xxiii.2.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Locke's distinction between primary and secondary qualities is not a claim about which perceptions are pleasant but a claim about which properties do **explanatory work**: solidity, extension, figure, motion and number are the properties a corpuscular physics must attribute to any body however small, while colour and warmth are powers relative to a perceiver — and it is because Locke adds to this defensible distinction a further **resemblance** thesis, and beneath it an admittedly unknown substratum, that Berkeley finds two targets rather than one.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **primary qualities** — solidity, extension, figure, motion or rest, number
- **secondary qualities** — colour, sound, taste, smell, warmth or cold
- **powers** — the exact word for secondary qualities
- **resemblance thesis** — ideas of primary qualities resemble; ideas of secondary qualities do not
- **inseparability test** — every fragment of a body retains the primary qualities
- **substratum** — "a supposition of he knows not what support of such qualities" (II.xxiii.2)

**How to use them:** give the list, then the **basis** of the distinction (inseparability plus explanatory role), then the resemblance thesis **separately**, because the resemblance thesis is what Berkeley refutes and the explanatory distinction is what survives him.

#### Plain-language visual — divide the sugar cube

```text
   TAKE A CUBE OF SUGAR AND CUT IT IN HALF. AGAIN. AND AGAIN.
   +------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | Every piece, however small, still has:                           |
   |    SOLIDITY   EXTENSION   FIGURE   MOTION OR REST   NUMBER       |
   |    -> these are PRIMARY QUALITIES: inseparable from body itself  |
   +------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | But its SWEETNESS is not "in" the fragment the way its shape is. |
   | Sweetness is the POWER of that shape-and-texture arrangement to  |
   | produce a taste in a creature with a tongue like ours.           |
   |    -> COLOUR, SOUND, TASTE, SMELL, WARMTH are SECONDARY          |
   +------------------------------------------------------------------+
                                |
   AND UNDERNEATH? Locke says: something must SUPPORT these qualities.
   But when he looks for it he finds no idea at all -
   only "a supposition of he knows not what support of such qualities".
```

**In simple words:** the test is not "can I sense it?" but "would a body still have it if I sliced it to invisibility?". Shape survives the slicing; sweetness does not, because sweetness was always a relation to a taster. The substratum question is different again: it asks what **has** the shape — and Locke's honest answer is that he does not know.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Primary | inseparable from body; enter physical explanation |
| Secondary | **powers** in the object to produce sensations in us |
| Resemblance | primary-quality ideas resemble; secondary-quality ideas do not |
| Substratum | supposed support of co-occurring qualities |
| "I know not what" | its **nature** is unknown; its existence is not denied |
| Not idealism | Locke never says the qualities are all in the mind |

> **One-line memory:** primary qualities **explain**; secondary qualities are **powers**; the substratum is a **posit**, not an observation.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the two-quality table, the two claims, and the two attacks they invite

```text
   THE TABLE
   =====================================================================
   PRIMARY QUALITIES               |  SECONDARY QUALITIES
   solidity, extension, figure,    |  colour, sound, taste, smell,
   motion/rest, number             |  warmth/cold
   really IN the object            |  POWERS in the object to produce
                                   |  sensations in us
   our ideas of them RESEMBLE      |  our ideas do NOT resemble
   the qualities themselves        |  anything in the object
   inseparable from body: every    |  depend on the perceiver's
   fragment retains them           |  sense-organs
   =====================================================================
                     |                             |
        CLAIM A: an EXPLANATORY claim    CLAIM B: a RESEMBLANCE claim
        (which properties a physics      (ideas of the first group
         must attribute to bodies)        picture their causes)
                     |                             |
                     |                             v
                     |            BERKELEY'S TWO ATTACKS
                     |     1 PARITY (Principles Sec.9-15): every
                     |       relativity argument used against secondary
                     |       qualities - variation with observer, organ,
                     |       medium - applies to primary ones too:
                     |       apparent size with distance, motion with
                     |       frame, shape with angle
                     |     2 LIKENESS (Sec.8): "an idea can be like
                     |       nothing but an idea"
                     v
        WHAT SURVIVES: the explanatory distinction, restated as
        measurable-structural versus response-dependent properties -
        which is why physics still uses it and Locke's version does not.
   =====================================================================
   BENEATH BOTH GROUPS: THE SUBSTRATUM
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | We meet clusters that regularly go together - yellow + heavy +     |
   | malleable = "gold" - and SUPPOSE a support for them.               |
   | Essay II.xxiii.2: "a supposition of he knows not what support of   |
   | such qualities."                                                   |
   | LOCKE DENIES KNOWLEDGE OF ITS NATURE, NOT ITS EXISTENCE.           |
   | Berkeley's move: an unknowable support is an ABSTRACT GENERAL IDEA |
   | (being + supporting), so if abstraction fails, "matter" is not     |
   | mysterious but MEANINGLESS.                                        |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

⚠️ **Caption.** Separating Claim A from Claim B is the single highest-value move in the 2024 answer. Berkeley destroys Claim B and merely embarrasses Claim A; a script that treats them as one claim cannot explain why the distinction survives in modern science.

#### 1. The lists, and the basis of the division

| Primary qualities | Secondary qualities |
|---|---|
| Solidity, extension, figure, motion or rest, number | Colour, sound, taste, smell, warmth or cold |
| Really *in* the object | **Powers** in the object to produce sensations in us |
| Our ideas of them **resemble** the qualities themselves | Our ideas do **not** resemble anything in the object |
| Inseparable from body — every fragment retains them | Depend on the perceiver's sense-organs |

✅ **Basis:** primary qualities are those that *any* body must have no matter how small; they are objective in the sense of entering the explanation of what bodies do. Secondary qualities are **relational** — the result of primary-quality configurations *acting on* our sense-organs.

#### 2. The word that keeps the doctrine accurate

⚠️ Secondary qualities are **powers**, not illusions and not "things in the mind". The sugar really does have the power to taste sweet to us; what it does not have is a sweetness resembling our sensation. Writing "secondary qualities are subjective" without the word *power* is the most common way of misstating Locke in this item.

#### 3. Real essence, again — the link to Session 4

- ✅ Behind the primary qualities lies the **real essence**, the arrangement of insensible corpuscles that produces all observable properties. We know *that* things have internal constitutions; we do not know *what* they are.
- ⚠️ So Locke's physics-friendly distinction is combined with a confession of ignorance about the very level at which the explanation would have to be completed. That combination is the honest shape of his empiricism, and it is what makes him vulnerable rather than incoherent.

#### 4. The substratum — stated exactly, and defended fairly

- ✅ We experience clusters of qualities that regularly go together — yellow, heavy, malleable, soluble in aqua regia — and suppose there is an underlying **substratum** that *supports* them.
- ✅ We have **no positive idea** of what this substratum is: it is "a supposition of he knows not what support of such qualities" (*Essay* II.xxiii.2).
- ⚠️ **Locke does not deny that substance exists.** He denies that we can know its nature. This is representative realism with an epistemic ceiling, not idealism and not scepticism.
- ⚠️ **The fairest defence:** the substratum is an **explanatory posit** tied to the stability of qualities and kinds, not a claimed object of intuition. Qualities do not float; something is qualified. Berkeley's reply is that "support" here is a dead metaphor borrowed from pillars and floors, and that stripping the metaphor leaves the abstract idea of *being in general* plus the relative notion of *supporting* — which is precisely an abstract general idea.

#### 5. The two attacks, and the exact adjudication

| Attack | Berkeley's form | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| **Parity** (*Principles* §§9–15; *Dialogues* I) | Extension cannot be conceived without colour; motion and rest are relative to the observer; figure is inseparable from extension. Every relativity argument Locke accepts for secondary qualities applies to primary ones | ⚠️ **Objection to the objection:** perceptual relativity does not entail mind-dependence. That a thing looks different from different positions is exactly what one should expect of a mind-*independent* thing with a real shape |
| **Likeness** (*Principles* §8) | "An idea can be like nothing but an idea"; and if the supposed originals are unperceivable, no comparison is even possible, so a resemblance-claim we could never in principle check is empty | ✅ **This lands.** The qualitative resemblance thesis is indefensible. **But** structural or mathematical resemblance — isomorphism — does not require qualitative likeness: a map resembles terrain without being terrain-coloured. That is the standard modern rescue of Locke |

> ⚠️ **Executable verdict:** the distinction survives as a distinction between *measurable-structural* and *response-dependent* properties, but not as Locke's **resemblance** thesis. Locke's mistake was to make the distinction depend on a picture rather than on explanatory role; reconstructed as a claim about which properties enter physical explanation, it is defensible — which is why it survives in science while Locke's version does not.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Locke's quality distinction contains two separable theses, and only one of them is destroyed by Berkeley.
> **Named evidence.** *Essay* II.viii lists solidity, extension, figure, motion or rest and number as inseparable from body while classifying colour, sound, taste, smell and warmth as powers to produce sensations, and adds that ideas of the first group resemble their qualities while ideas of the second resemble nothing; II.xxiii.2 then places both on a substratum that is only "a supposition of he knows not what support of such qualities".
> **Analysis.** Berkeley's parity argument at *Principles* §§9–15 shows only that primary qualities are perceptually relative, which a realist can accept, whereas the likeness principle at §8 shows that a resemblance between an idea and an unperceivable quality could never be checked and is therefore empty — so the explanatory thesis stands and the pictorial thesis falls.
> **Qualification.** What survives is a distinction between measurable-structural and response-dependent properties defended by isomorphism rather than by likeness, and Locke's substratum survives only as an explanatory posit whose intelligibility depends on the doctrine of abstraction that Berkeley attacks first.

#### Exam link

- **2024 Q2(a), 20 marks** — "Is rejection of Locke's notion of primary qualities instrumental in Berkeley's leaning towards idealism?" This session is the first limb; the answer is **yes, but through the likeness principle and the anti-abstraction argument, not through relativity alone**.
- **2021 Q1(c), 10 marks** — Berkeley's "immediately perceived" presupposes the collapse of the quality distinction.
- Any "substance" question in this item begins from §4.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Secondary qualities are illusions" | They are **powers**; the object really has them, relative to perceivers |
| "Locke denies substance" | He denies **knowledge of its nature**; he affirms its existence |
| Treating relativity as decisive | Relativity alone does not entail mind-dependence; the likeness principle is the sharp weapon |
| Merging the explanatory and resemblance claims | Only the resemblance claim falls; say which |
| Quoting the substratum phrase loosely | The words are "a supposition of he knows not what support of such qualities" (II.xxiii.2) |

#### Revision notes

- Primary: solidity, extension, figure, motion or rest, number — inseparable, explanatory.
- Secondary: colour, sound, taste, smell, warmth or cold — **powers** relative to perceivers.
- Resemblance thesis: primary ideas resemble; secondary ideas do not.
- Substratum II.xxiii.2: "a supposition of he knows not what support of such qualities".
- Berkeley: parity (§§9–15) plus likeness (§8); likeness is the one that lands.
- Survives: measurable-structural versus response-dependent, defended by isomorphism.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — LOCKE IV: QUALITIES AND SUBSTANCE

