```text
QUALITY = THE POWER IN A BODY TO PRODUCE AN IDEA IN US
        |
        +--> PRIMARY: solidity, extension, figure, motion/rest, number
        |    inseparable from body; ideas RESEMBLE; enter physical explanation
        +--> SECONDARY: colour, sound, taste, smell, warmth/cold
             POWERS grounded in primary configurations; ideas resemble nothing
        |
        v
TWO SEPARABLE THESES: (A) EXPLANATORY DIVISION   (B) RESEMBLANCE PICTURE
        |
        +--> BERKELEY PARITY (Sec.9-15) attacks A -> answered: relativity is
        |    what a real shape would produce
        +--> BERKELEY LIKENESS (Sec.8) attacks B -> LANDS: an unverifiable
             resemblance is empty; rescue = isomorphism, not likeness
        |
        v
BENEATH BOTH: SUBSTRATUM = "a supposition of he knows not what support of
such qualities" (II.xxiii.2) - NATURE unknown, EXISTENCE not denied
        |
        v
BERKELEY'S NEXT STEP: an unknowable support is an ABSTRACT GENERAL IDEA;
kill abstraction and "matter" becomes meaningless, not mysterious
TRAP: illusions | denial of substance | relativity as decisive
ANSWER LINE: the explanatory distinction survives; the picture does not
```

### SESSION 6 — LOCKE V: PERSONAL IDENTITY AS CONTINUITY OF CONSCIOUSNESS, THE OBJECTIONS, AND THE DEMONSTRATIVE PROOF OF GOD

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** For Locke, what makes you the same person as the child in an old photograph is not the same body and not the same soul but the fact that your present consciousness can reach back and claim that child's experiences as your own — which is why "person" is fundamentally a term about responsibility.

**Technical definition:** Locke's account of personal identity, added at *Essay* II.xxvii in the second edition of 1694, distinguishes the identity of a **substance**, a **human animal** and a **person**; personal identity consists in the extension of **consciousness** backwards over past thoughts and actions, with **memory** as its chief vehicle and evidence, making "person" a **forensic** term tied to accountability.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Locke's most radical move on personal identity is not the appeal to memory but the prior separation of three questions that his predecessors ran together: the identity of a substance, of a living human organism, and of a person are three different identities with three different criteria, and once "person" is defined as "a thinking intelligent being that can consider itself as itself in different times and places", identity of person must be settled by consciousness — which is why the doctrine is best judged as a theory of accountability rather than as a theory of metaphysical substance.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **person, human animal (man), substance** — three identities, three criteria
- **continuity of consciousness** — the constitutive notion
- **memory as vehicle and evidence** — not as the definition
- **appropriation** — consciousness claiming a past act as its own
- **forensic** — Locke's own word for the concept of person
- **prince and cobbler** — the case that separates person from man
- **circularity (Butler, Reid)** and the **Brave Officer** (Reid)

**How to use them:** state the three-fold distinction first, then define person, then the criterion, then the prince-and-cobbler case, then the two objections and Locke's repair. A script that opens with "memory theory" and never separates person from man has already lost the structure of the answer.

#### Plain-language visual — three questions people confuse

```text
   THE SAME WHAT?
   +---------------------------+-------------------------------------+
   | SAME SUBSTANCE            | is it the same underlying stuff,    |
   |                           | material or immaterial?             |
   +---------------------------+-------------------------------------+
   | SAME MAN (human animal)   | is it the same continuing living    |
   |                           | organism?                           |
   +---------------------------+-------------------------------------+
   | SAME PERSON               | can this present consciousness      |
   |                           | APPROPRIATE that past thought or    |
   |                           | action as its own?                  |
   +---------------------------+-------------------------------------+

   THE PRINCE AND THE COBBLER
   prince's consciousness  --transferred into-->  cobbler's body
   RESULT: the PERSON who wakes up is the prince
           the MAN who wakes up is the cobbler
   THEREFORE: personal identity is neither bodily nor substantial identity.
```

**In simple words:** Locke's question is not "what am I made of?" but "whose deeds are mine?". Punishment, praise, promise-keeping and reward all presuppose that a present self can own a past act. Locke builds the concept of person to fit that job, and then follows the criterion wherever it leads — even into a thought-experiment where a prince wakes up in a cobbler's body.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Person | "a thinking intelligent being, that has reason and reflection, and can consider itself as itself, the same thinking thing, in different times and places" (II.xxvii.9) |
| Criterion | extension of consciousness, not sameness of substance |
| Memory | the vehicle and evidence of that extension |
| Forensic | a term of law and morals, tied to accountability |
| 1694 | the chapter is a **second-edition addition** |

> **One-line memory:** same substance, same animal and same person are **three** questions; only the third is settled by consciousness.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the criterion, the two objections and the repair

```text
   LOCKE'S CRITERION                                   Essay II.xxvii
   =====================================================================
   PRESENT CONSCIOUSNESS -----appropriates-----> PAST THOUGHT OR ACTION
        |                                                   |
        +--- as far as this reaches, the PERSON extends -----+
   MEMORY is how the reach is exercised and evidenced, but the
   CONSTITUTIVE notion is CONSCIOUSNESS. Reducing the theory to bare
   memory INTENSIFIES the circularity problem - do not do it.
   =====================================================================
                                |
   OBJECTION 1 - CIRCULARITY (Butler, Reid)
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | I can remember only MY OWN experiences. So "genuine memory"        |
   | already presupposes identity. If identity is then DEFINED by       |
   | memory, the definition is circular.                                |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   REPLY: distinguish the CONSTITUTIVE criterion (consciousness) from the
   EVIDENTIAL criterion (memory). Contested - the objector answers that
   "consciousness of X as mine" smuggles the same ownership back in.
                                |
   OBJECTION 2 - TRANSITIVITY: REID'S BRAVE OFFICER
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   |   BOY (flogged at school)  <--remembers--  YOUNG OFFICER           |
   |   YOUNG OFFICER            <--remembers--  OLD GENERAL             |
   |   BOY                      <--NOT remembered by-- OLD GENERAL      |
   |                                                                    |
   |   So: boy = officer, officer = general, but boy =/= general.       |
   |   Identity is transitive. Locke's criterion is not.                |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   REPAIR: OVERLAPPING CHAINS of consciousness - identity is the
   ANCESTRAL of direct appropriation. Locke's criterion is thereby saved
   at the price of no longer being a simple episodic-memory criterion.
                                |
   OBJECTION 3 - GAPS: dreamless sleep, anaesthesia, amnesia
   REPLY: continuous CAPACITY for appropriation, not constant recollection.
   RESIDUAL: capacity-talk strains the account, and Locke concedes that
   whether the same substance persists through a gap is unknowable to us.
   =====================================================================
```

⚠️ **Caption.** The examiner's word in 2021 Q1(d) is "**Examine**". That directive requires the doctrine **and** the test; an answer that states the criterion and stops has performed only half the instruction.

#### 1. The definition, and why it is built the way it is

✅ A person is "**a thinking intelligent being, that has reason and reflection, and can consider itself as itself, the same thinking thing, in different times and places**" (*Essay* II.xxvii.9). Self-reflexive awareness is written into the definition, so the identity condition must be an awareness-condition.

#### 2. The three identities, kept apart

- ✅ **Same substance** — sameness of the material or immaterial bearer. Unknowable to us in the immaterial case.
- ✅ **Same man (human animal)** — participation in one continued life of the same organised living body.
- ✅ **Same person** — the extension of present consciousness over past thoughts and actions.
- ⚠️ Locke's radicalism is precisely this **separation**. It is what allows him to say that the same man may be two persons, or the same person two men — and it is what makes a purely bodily or purely substantial criterion unavailable.

#### 3. Consciousness, memory and appropriation

- ✅ Personal identity extends as far as present consciousness can **appropriate** a past thought or action as its own.
- ✅ **Memory is the chief mechanism and evidence** of this backward extension, but the constitutive notion is consciousness. ⚠️ Reducing the theory to bare memory intensifies the circularity problem, so the precise formulation is itself a defensive move.
- ✅ **Forensic concept:** Locke ties personal identity to **moral responsibility** — we are accountable only for acts we can appropriate. "Person" belongs, in his own words, to law and to reward and punishment.

#### 4. The prince and the cobbler

✅ If the prince's consciousness, carrying the memory of his princely life, enters and informs the cobbler's body, then the **person** is the prince while the **man** is the cobbler. The case is decisive because it shows in one stroke that personal identity is neither bodily nor substantial identity, and it does so without requiring any metaphysical commitment about souls.

#### 5. The objections, and the honest verdict

| Objection | Source | Force | Best reply |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Circularity** | Butler, Reid (18th c.) | Memory presupposes identity — I can remember only *my own* experiences, so identity must already be settled before memory can confirm it | Distinguish the **constitutive** criterion (consciousness) from the **evidential** criterion (memory). ❓ Contested: "consciousness of it *as mine*" may reintroduce ownership |
| **Transitivity failure — the Brave Officer** | Reid (1710–1796) | Boy → young officer → old general. The officer remembers the boy; the general remembers the officer but not the boy. By the criterion, general ≠ boy, yet officer = boy and general = officer | **Overlapping chains**: identity is the ancestral of direct appropriation. ⚠️ Saves transitivity, but abandons the simple episodic reading |
| **Gaps in consciousness** | Critics generally | Dreamless sleep and amnesia would interrupt the person | Continuous **capacity** for appropriation rather than constant recollection. ⚠️ This strains the account, and Locke himself admits the substance question is undecidable for us |

> ⚠️ **Executable verdict:** Locke's theory is best defended as a **repaired psychological-continuity** account, and on that reading it survives Reid; but the repaired version is no longer the simple memory criterion the textbooks attribute to him, and the circularity charge remains genuinely open. Saying exactly this is what "examine" requires.

#### 6. Locke on God — the demonstrative proof

- ✅ God's existence is known by **demonstration** — not by intuition and not by sensitive knowledge. The chain runs: something cannot come from nothing; I exist and I know that I exist with intuitive certainty; therefore there has existed from eternity a most powerful and most knowing being.
- ⚠️ It is a **cosmological-style** argument conducted inside an empiricist framework, and it is consistent with Locke's own ladder: God is reached at the **second** degree of knowledge, above the external world and below self-knowledge.
- ⚠️ **The exposed premise** is the causal principle that nothing comes from nothing — precisely the principle Hume will later refuse to certify, because there is no impression of causal necessity. This is the cleanest single link between Locke's God and Hume's scepticism, and it belongs in any comparative answer on God in this item.
- ❌ There is no innate idea of God for Locke; that was one of the candidates Book I dismissed.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Locke's theory of personal identity is a theory of accountability, and it survives its classic objections only in a repaired form.
> **Named evidence.** *Essay* II.xxvii, added in the second edition of 1694, defines a person as a thinking intelligent being that can consider itself as itself in different times and places, separates the identity of person from that of man and of substance, illustrates the separation with the prince and the cobbler, and calls "person" a forensic term.
> **Analysis.** Butler's and Reid's circularity charge and Reid's Brave Officer case both attack the criterion where it is stated as bare episodic memory, so the defensible Lockean position must locate identity in consciousness rather than recollection and must define it through overlapping chains of appropriation rather than through single acts of remembering.
> **Qualification.** That repair preserves transitivity at the cost of the simple memory criterion, and it does not silence the circularity objection, since consciousness *of a past act as mine* may already presuppose the identity it is meant to constitute — which is why the honest verdict grades the theory as forensically indispensable and metaphysically incomplete.

#### Exam link

- **2021 Q1(d), 10 marks** — "Examine the concept of personal identity by Locke." Doctrine from §§1–4, objections from §5, and a graded verdict.
- **2020 Q2(a), 20 marks** — Locke's account is the position Hume dismantles; the contrast belongs in the first paragraph of that answer.
- Locke's God in §6 supplies the comparative material for any "God in the empiricists" question.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| Calling it simply "the memory theory" | The constitutive notion is **consciousness**; memory is vehicle and evidence |
| Ignoring the person/man/substance distinction | It is the structural core of the chapter |
| Dating the doctrine to 1690 | II.xxvii is a **1694** second-edition addition |
| Reporting objections without a reply | "Examine" requires the repair and a verdict |
| Saying Locke has an innate idea of God | Book I explicitly denies it; the proof is **demonstrative** |

#### Revision notes

- Person defined at II.xxvii.9; chapter added 1694.
- Three identities: substance · man (living organism) · person.
- Criterion: extension of consciousness; memory is vehicle and evidence; appropriation is the key verb.
- Prince and cobbler: person follows consciousness, man follows organism.
- Objections: Butler/Reid circularity; Reid's Brave Officer transitivity; gaps.
- Repair: overlapping chains; capacity rather than constant recollection.
- God: demonstrative, cosmological-style; rests on the causal principle Hume later attacks.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — LOCKE V: SELF AND GOD

```text
QUESTION: WHAT MAKES THE PERSON AT T2 THE SAME AS THE PERSON AT T1?
        |
        v
SEPARATE THREE IDENTITIES: SUBSTANCE | MAN (organism) | PERSON
        |
        v
PERSON (II.xxvii.9, added 1694) = thinking intelligent being that can
consider itself as itself in different times and places
        |
        v
CRITERION: present consciousness APPROPRIATES a past act
   memory = vehicle and evidence, NOT the definition
   FORENSIC: the concept is built for accountability
        |
        +--> PRINCE AND COBBLER: person = prince, man = cobbler
        |
        +--> BUTLER/REID CIRCULARITY -> constitutive vs evidential (contested)
        +--> REID'S BRAVE OFFICER -> OVERLAPPING CHAINS restore transitivity
        +--> GAPS -> continuous CAPACITY, not constant recollection
        |
        v
GOD: demonstrative, not intuitive or sensitive; nothing comes from nothing;
I exist -> an eternal most powerful and most knowing being
   EXPOSED PREMISE: the causal principle Hume will refuse to certify
TRAP: "memory theory" | 1690 dating | omitting the objections
ANSWER LINE: forensically indispensable, metaphysically incomplete
```

### SESSION 7 — BERKELEY I: THE DESTRUCTION OF ABSTRACTION AND THE NOMINALIST REPLACEMENT

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Berkeley says you cannot form an idea of a colourless extension or of a triangle that is no particular triangle; what you can do is take one perfectly ordinary particular idea and let it stand for all the others of its kind — and once you accept that, the word "matter" turns out to name nothing at all.

**Technical definition:** Berkeley's **nominalism** denies the existence of abstract general ideas while affirming general **ideas**: an idea becomes general not by being stripped of determinations but "by being made to represent or stand for all other particular ideas of the same sort" (*Principles*, Introduction §12), and since Locke's *material substance in general* is precisely an abstract general idea, the failure of abstraction makes "matter" a **meaningless expression** rather than an unknown thing.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Berkeley's nominalism is not a side-issue in his system but its foundation: he places the attack on abstract general ideas in the **Introduction** to the *Principles*, before a single word about matter, because his immaterialism is a corollary of it — Locke's "material substance in general" is the abstract idea of being conjoined with the relative notion of supporting, so if no such idea can be framed, matter is not a mysterious unknown but an expression without content.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **separation of what can exist apart** — the abstraction Berkeley allows
- **abstraction proper** — separating what cannot exist apart; this he denies
- **"all and none of these at once"** — Locke's triangle, quoted at Intro §13
- **"made to represent or stand for all other particular ideas of the same sort"** (Intro §12)
- **selective attention** — the positive mechanism
- **language does not always stand for ideas** (Intro §§19–20)
- **matter as an abstract general idea** — the metaphysical payload

**How to use them:** run the seven numbered steps, then give the positive replacement with the geometer example, then the payload — and only then move to *to be is to be perceived*. The order matters: abstraction first, matter second.

#### Plain-language visual — two kinds of "separating"

```text
   WHAT BERKELEY ADMITS HE CAN DO           WHAT HE SAYS NOBODY CAN DO
   +-------------------------------+        +----------------------------+
   | Imagine a HAND without the    |        | Imagine EXTENSION with no   |
   | rest of the body.             |        | colour at all.              |
   | Imagine a HEADLESS torso.     |        | Imagine MOTION with no      |
   |                               |        | determinate speed.          |
   | These parts COULD exist       |        | Imagine A MAN who has no    |
   | apart, so the separation is   |        | stature and no complexion.  |
   | legitimate.                   |        | These CANNOT exist apart,   |
   |                               |        | so the "idea" is empty.     |
   +-------------------------------+        +----------------------------+
                            \               /
                             v             v
              AND LOCKE'S TRIANGLE IS THE WORST CASE:
              "neither oblique nor rectangle, neither equilateral,
               equicrural, nor scalenon; but all and none of these
               at once."  -> that is not an idea; it is a contradiction.
```

**In simple words:** Berkeley is not saying imagination is weak. He is saying that "separating" only makes sense where the separated items could exist on their own. You can picture a hand by itself because hands can be severed; you cannot picture extension by itself because nothing extended is colourless. Then he turns to Locke's own sentence and lets it do the work.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Allowed | separating parts that could exist apart |
| Denied | separating what cannot exist apart |
| The quotation | Locke's triangle, at *Principles* Intro §13 |
| Replacement | a determinate particular idea **made to stand for** a sort |
| Mechanism | selective attention to a respect of resemblance |
| Payload | "matter" is an abstract general idea; so it means nothing |

> **One-line memory:** generality lives in the **use of a sign**, not in a stripped-down picture.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the seven steps, the replacement and the payload

```text
   BERKELEY'S DEMOLITION, NUMBERED         Principles, Introduction 6-25
   =====================================================================
   1 I concede I can imagine particular things I have perceived, and
     compound and divide such ideas - a hand without a body, a headless
     torso. This is SEPARATION OF PARTS THAT COULD EXIST APART.
   2 But abstraction PROPER requires separating what CANNOT exist apart:
     extension without any colour; motion without a determinate speed;
     "man" without any determinate stature or complexion.
   3 Locke's own triangle - neither equilateral nor scalene, yet all and
     none at once - is SELF-CONTRADICTORY; no such idea can be framed.
     (Berkeley quotes the passage VERBATIM at Intro Sec.13.)
   4 THEREFORE there are no abstract general ideas.
   5 POSITIVE REPLACEMENT: an idea is general not by being abstract but
     "by being made to represent or stand for all other particular ideas
     of the same sort" (Intro Sec.12). A determinate particular idea,
     SELECTIVELY ATTENDED TO, does duty for a class: the geometer draws
     THIS black inch-long line and proves a theorem about ALL lines.
   6 COROLLARY AGAINST THE WHOLE TRADITION: words do not always stand for
     ideas at all; language also raises passion, prompts action and
     disposes the mind (Intro Sec.19-20).
   7 METAPHYSICAL PAYLOAD: Locke's MATERIAL SUBSTANCE IN GENERAL and
     SUBSTRATUM are precisely abstract general ideas - the abstract idea
     of BEING plus the relative notion of SUPPORTING. If abstraction is
     impossible, "matter" is not a mysterious unknown but a MEANINGLESS
     EXPRESSION. Immaterialism therefore RESTS ON the nominalism.
   =====================================================================
                                |
   THE COUNTER-PRESSURE YOU MUST ALSO KNOW
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | If a particular idea stands for a class, what fixes WHICH respect  |
   | it is attended to under? The resemblance-respect looks like the    |
   | abstract idea returning under another name. This is the standard   |
   | modern rejoinder, and it makes the dispute far less one-sided than |
   | textbooks suggest.                                                 |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

⚠️ **Caption.** Step 6 is the step almost no script contains, and it is a genuine anticipation of the later Wittgensteinian point that meaning is use. Flagging it converts a descriptive answer on nominalism into one that shows the argument's reach.

#### 1. What the doctrine denies, and what it does not

- ✅ Berkeley denies **abstract general ideas**; he does not deny **general ideas**, general words, or general knowledge. Geometry proceeds perfectly well on his account.
- ❌ He is therefore not a "nominalist" in the crude sense of denying that classification is possible. His nominalism is about the **vehicle** of generality, not about its legitimacy.

#### 2. The argument's dependence on the imagistic reading

⚠️ Step 3 works because Locke's idea is treated as an **image**, and an image cannot be indeterminate. Locke's best reply — that the abstract idea is **partial** rather than **contradictory**, with the angles left undetermined instead of contradictorily assigned — was set out in Session 4. Berkeley's argument therefore proves what it proves **only against imagistic Locke**, and a first-class answer says so.

#### 3. The positive account, and its unpaid bill

- ✅ **Selective attention**: I consider *this* determinate triangle only in respect of its three-sidedness, and my proof is valid for all triangles because nothing in it depends on the features I ignored.
- ❓ **The bill:** what fixes the respect? If the answer is "a further idea of the respect", the abstract idea has returned; if the answer is "a practice of use", Berkeley has moved beyond the theory of ideas altogether — which is exactly the direction step 6 points.

#### 4. Why this is the base of immaterialism

✅ The order of Berkeley's own book is the order of his argument. Abstraction is destroyed in the Introduction so that in §§1–4 the identification of a sensible thing with a collection of ideas cannot be resisted by appeal to an abstract "something" underlying them. **Berkeley's immaterialism rests on his nominalism, not the reverse** — say this sentence in the 2025 answer and the whole structure becomes visible.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Berkeley's refutation of abstract ideas is the foundation of his immaterialism rather than an independent thesis about psychology.
> **Named evidence.** In the Introduction to the *Principles* he concedes the separation of parts that could exist apart, denies the separation of what cannot, quotes Locke's own admission at IV.vii.9 that the general triangle is "all and none" of the determinate triangles, replaces abstraction with a determinate idea "made to represent or stand for all other particular ideas of the same sort" at §12, and observes at §§19–20 that words need not stand for ideas at all.
> **Analysis.** Because Locke's *material substance in general* is compounded from the abstract idea of being and the relative notion of supporting, the collapse of abstraction converts matter from an unknown thing into an expression with no content, which is why the attack precedes the metaphysics in Berkeley's own order of exposition.
> **Qualification.** The argument succeeds only against an imagistic conception of ideas, and Berkeley's positive account owes an explanation of what fixes the respect under which a particular idea represents its class — so the durable casualty is the ideational theory of meaning shared by both men rather than generality itself.

#### Exam link

- **2025 Q4(c), 15 marks** — "Explain Berkeley's doctrine of nominalism and his refutation of Abstract ideas." This session is the answer; Session 4 supplies the doctrine refuted.
- **2024 Q2(a), 20 marks** — the payload in §4 is why rejecting Locke's primary qualities is *instrumental* rather than merely contributory.
- **2018 Q3(a), 20 marks** — "Mind and its ideas alone are real" is unintelligible without the anti-abstraction ground clearing.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Berkeley denies general ideas" | He denies **abstract** general ideas; particular ideas made to stand for a sort are general |
| Presenting nominalism after immaterialism | Berkeley's own order is the reverse, and the order is the argument |
| Giving the conclusion without the steps | "Explain" demands the derivation, not a synonym for the conclusion |
| Omitting §§19–20 | The claim that words need not stand for ideas is the argument's longest reach |
| Treating the refutation as complete | The determinable reading of Locke and the respect-of-representation problem both survive |

#### Revision notes

- Allowed: separating parts that could exist apart. Denied: separating what cannot.
- Locke's triangle quoted verbatim at *Principles* Intro §13.
- Replacement at §12: a particular idea "made to represent or stand for all other particular ideas of the same sort".
- Mechanism: selective attention; example: the geometer's inch-long line.
- §§19–20: words also raise passion, prompt action, dispose the mind.
- Payload: matter = abstract idea of being + relative notion of supporting → meaningless.
- Counter-pressure: what fixes the respect of representation?

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — BERKELEY I: ANTI-ABSTRACTION

```text
LOCKE: GENERALITY LIVES IN A STRIPPED-DOWN ABSTRACT IDEA
        |
        v
BERKELEY, PRINCIPLES INTRODUCTION Sec.6-25
   1 separation of parts that COULD exist apart: ALLOWED
   2 separation of what CANNOT exist apart: IMPOSSIBLE
   3 Locke's triangle "all and none" is self-contradictory (quoted, Sec.13)
   4 therefore NO abstract general ideas
   5 REPLACEMENT (Sec.12): a determinate particular idea "made to represent
     or stand for all other particular ideas of the same sort"
     - selective attention; the geometer's inch-long line
   6 words do not always stand for ideas at all (Sec.19-20)
   7 PAYLOAD: matter = abstract idea of BEING + relative notion of SUPPORTING
     -> not unknown but MEANINGLESS
        |
        v
IMMATERIALISM RESTS ON NOMINALISM, NOT THE REVERSE
        |
        +--> LIMIT 1: works only against IMAGISTIC Locke (determinable reply)
        +--> LIMIT 2: what fixes the RESPECT of representation?
TRAP: "denies general ideas" | wrong order | conclusion without derivation
ANSWER LINE: generality lives in the use of a sign, not in a stripped picture
```

### SESSION 8 — BERKELEY II: THE FIVE ARGUMENTS FOR IMMATERIALISM AND WHAT "TO BE IS TO BE PERCEIVED" CLAIMS

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Berkeley holds that a chair is nothing more than the collection of sights, sounds and feels you get from it, that for such things existing just *is* being perceived, and that the extra "matter" philosophers place behind them is an empty word — so the chair is exactly as real as it ever was, and only the philosopher's ghost has been removed.

**Technical definition:** Berkeley's **immaterialism** or **subjective idealism** holds that sensible things are collections of ideas immediately perceived, that for sensible things *to be is to be perceived (esse est percipi)* (*Principles* §3), and that "material substance" — an unperceiving, unperceivable substratum — is an incoherent abstraction; reality consists of **spirits** and their **ideas**.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> *To be is to be perceived* is a conclusion, not a slogan, and the difference between an average and a first-class answer on Berkeley is whether the five supporting arguments are named: the **semantic** identification of sensible things with ideas, the **likeness** principle, the **parity** collapse of the quality distinction, the **Master Argument** about conceiving the unconceived, and the argument from **continuity** to an infinite spirit — five arguments where the average script asserts one Latin tag.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **sensible things** — things immediately perceived by sense
- **collection of ideas** — the identification that carries the system
- **to be is to be perceived (esse est percipi)** — *Principles* §3, Berkeley's own Latin
- **likeness principle** — "an idea can be like nothing but an idea" (§8)
- **parity argument** — relativity applies equally to primary qualities (§§9–15)
- **Master Argument** — conceive an unconceived body (§§22–23)
- **absolute existence without relation to being perceived** — the phrase Berkeley calls unintelligible

**How to use them:** name all five arguments, give the presupposition of each, and give the strongest objection to at least two. That structure alone answers 2018 Q3(a) and 2021 Q1(c) and half of 2024 Q2(a).

#### Plain-language visual — what is actually being denied

```text
   THE ORDINARY PERSON'S PICTURE          THE PHILOSOPHER'S ADDITION
   +-----------------------------+        +--------------------------+
   | I see the table.            |        | Behind the table there   |
   | I feel the table.           |        | is MATTER: an unthinking,|
   | The table is there.         |        | unperceivable substance  |
   +-----------------------------+        | that "supports" the      |
                                          | qualities and causes my  |
   BERKELEY KEEPS ALL OF THIS   <-------- | ideas.                   |
                                          +--------------------------+
                                                     |
                                          BERKELEY DELETES ONLY THIS
                                                     |
   RESULT: "The table I write on, I say, exists; that is, I see and
   feel it."  (Principles Sec.3)  The table is not less real. The
   philosopher's ghost behind it is gone - and with it, Berkeley
   claims, the scepticism that ghost generated.
```

**In simple words:** Berkeley thinks the materialist invented an unknowable world and then worried about whether we can reach it. Remove the unknowable world and the worry disappears. This is why he calls himself the defender of common sense and calls the materialist the sceptic — a reversal that must be stated in any answer or the position will be misdescribed.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Sensible thing | what is **immediately** perceived by sense |
| The identification | an apple, a stone, a tree, a book = collections of ideas |
| *Esse est percipi* | for **sensible things**, existence = being perceived |
| What is denied | matter as an unperceiving substratum |
| What is kept | tables, chairs, the order of nature, science, other minds, God |
| Direction of attack | the materialist is the sceptic, not Berkeley |

> **One-line memory:** Berkeley deletes the **substratum**, never the **table**.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — five arguments, five presuppositions, three objections

```text
   ARGUMENT 1 - SEMANTIC / ANALYTIC                 Principles Sec.1-4
   1 The objects of human knowledge are ideas: (a) imprinted on the
     senses, (b) perceived by attending to the passions and operations
     of the mind, (c) formed by memory and imagination.
   2 "Sensible things" MEANS things immediately perceived by sense.
   3 An apple, a stone, a tree, a book are nothing but collections of
     such immediately perceived ideas.
   4 It is impossible that ideas should exist unperceived - their esse
     is percipi.
   5 THEREFORE for sensible things esse est percipi, and "absolute
     existence without relation to their being perceived" is
     unintelligible.
   PRESUPPOSITION: step 3 is a DEFINITION, not a discovery. Grant it and
   immaterialism follows in four lines; deny it and nothing follows.
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   ARGUMENT 2 - THE LIKENESS PRINCIPLE                  Principles Sec.8
   1 Locke says ideas of primary qualities RESEMBLE qualities in bodies.
   2 But "an idea can be like nothing but an idea" - a colour or figure
     can be like nothing but another colour or figure.
   3 If the supposed originals are unperceivable, no comparison with them
     is even possible; a resemblance we could never check is empty.
   4 THEREFORE the representative-realist resemblance is unintelligible.
   PRESUPPOSITION: resemblance requires comparability by a perceiver.
   OBJECTION: structural/mathematical resemblance (ISOMORPHISM) needs no
   qualitative likeness - a map resembles terrain without being
   terrain-coloured. This is the standard modern rescue of Locke.
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   ARGUMENT 3 - PARITY                        Principles Sec.9-15; Dial. I
   Every relativity argument Locke accepts for secondary qualities -
   variation with observer, with organ, with medium - applies equally to
   primary ones: apparent size with distance, motion with frame of
   reference, shape with angle. So ALL qualities are ideas, and nothing
   is left for "material substance" to be.
   PRESUPPOSITION: perceptual relativity entails mind-dependence.
   OBJECTION: it does not - looking different from different positions is
   what one should expect of a mind-INDEPENDENT thing with a real shape.
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   ARGUMENT 4 - THE MASTER ARGUMENT                 Principles Sec.22-23
   1 Conceive one extended movable body existing unconceived, and
     Berkeley concedes materialism.
   2 You reply: I easily conceive a tree standing alone in a park with
     nobody by.
   3 But in doing so YOU are conceiving it - you have framed ideas in
     your own mind and have not conceived the tree UNCONCEIVED at all.
   4 THEREFORE the supposition is self-refuting.
   PRESUPPOSITION (the fatal one): that conceiving "X existing unconceived"
   requires the act of conceiving to be part of the CONTENT conceived.
   OBJECTION (Russell; refined by Prior and Gallois): a SCOPE or
   use-mention confusion. What is inconceivable is "I conceive of X THAT
   IT IS NOT CONCEIVED BY ME"; what is required is only "I conceive THAT
   X exists and is not conceived" - perfectly coherent, exactly as I can
   think ABOUT an unthought-of number.
   BERKELEY'S BEST REPLY: for him there is no object over and above the
   idea, so the distinction the objector needs is precisely what is at
   issue - the argument begs the question only if immaterialism is
   already false.
   ---------------------------------------------------------------------
   ARGUMENT 5 - GOD AS GUARANTOR OF CONTINUITY  Sec.6, 30-33, 48; Dial. II
   1 Ideas are passive and inert; they cannot cause anything.
   2 Yet the ideas of sense come to me INVOLUNTARILY, in steady order.
   3 Whatever produces them must be an ACTIVE SPIRIT whose power and
     wisdom exceed mine.
   4 THEREFORE God. The "laws of nature" are the settled grammar of God's
     language of ideas: regularities are SIGNS, not causes.
   5 THEREFORE objects persist unperceived by finite minds because they
     are always perceived by the infinite Mind.
   PRESUPPOSITION: only spirits are active causes (Berkeley's voluntarism).
   OBJECTION: this makes "existence unperceived" a hypothesis about an
   unobserved being - the very move Berkeley forbade Locke.
   REPLY: spirit is known not by IDEA but by NOTION (Sec.27, 89, 140) - an
   asymmetry critics call ad hoc, but which Berkeley holds is forced,
   since an idea, being passive, could never picture activity.
```

⚠️ **Caption.** Naming the five arguments — semantic, likeness, parity, master, continuity — is the single largest available differentiator on this sub-topic. Add one presupposition and one objection and the answer is already in the top band.

#### 1. The formula, stated with its scope

- ✅ For **sensible things**, existence is being perceived: "The table I write on, I say, exists; that is, I see and feel it" (*Principles* §3).
- ⚠️ The scope restriction is not a technicality. *Esse est percipi* applies to **sensible ideas**; spirits are active perceivers and willers, and are known by **notion**, not as passive perceived ideas.
- ⚠️ **Provenance mark:** the expanded formula "to be is to be perceived or to perceive" (*esse est percipi aut percipere*) is a **later commentators' expansion** to accommodate spirits. Do not present it as Berkeley's quoted Latin.

#### 2. What immaterialism deletes and what it keeps

- ❌ Deleted: **material substance** — an unthinking, unperceiving, unperceivable substratum.
- ✅ Kept: tables and chairs (as orderly collections of ideas), the distinction between real things and imagination, natural law, science, other minds, and God.
- ✅ **Reality = minds (spirits) + their ideas.** This is subjective idealism, also called immaterialism.

#### 3. Berkeley "saves" common sense — the reversal

- ✅ Berkeley insists he is not denying the reality of tables and chairs; he denies only the **philosopher's fiction** of matter. The ordinary person never believed in Locke's veil of perception: they believe they perceive things *directly* — and so does Berkeley.
- ✅ He accuses the **materialists** of undermining common sense by positing an unknowable world behind appearances, and of thereby manufacturing the sceptical problem they then fail to solve.
- ⚠️ **Assessment worth including:** the defence is strong about *objects* and weaker about *causes*, because the plain man does think fire heats water, whereas for Berkeley fire is a sign and God is the cause.

#### 4. Illusion, science and other minds — the four objections he must answer

| Objection | Berkeley's answer |
|---|---|
| **What distinguishes reality from imagination?** | Ideas of sense are involuntary, vivid, coherent and law-governed; ideas of imagination are comparatively voluntary and irregular |
| **What about illusions — the bent oar, the distant tower?** | The presented idea genuinely exists; error lies in the **judgment** about which further ideas will follow, not in the existence of what is perceived |
| **Does this destroy science?** | No. Natural laws describe stable sequences in God's language of ideas and support prediction; what is denied is material efficient power behind them, not empirical regularity |
| **Does this leave only me — solipsism?** | Other finite spirits are not perceived as ideas but inferred by **analogy** from purposive signs and behaviour, while God explains the comprehensive involuntary order |

⚠️ Honest residual: this blocks crude solipsism, but knowledge of other spirits and of God is **less immediate** than awareness of one's own activity and of the ideas presented.

#### 5. Why Berkeley is an anti-sceptic

- ✅ He claims to **refute** scepticism: it is the materialist who produces doubt by positing an unknowable world behind ideas. By identifying reality with ideas immediately perceived, Berkeley claims certainty, and God guarantees continuity.
- ✅ His idealism is therefore **dogmatic theistic idealism** — a metaphysical thesis about what exists, not a doubt about knowledge.
- ❌ Never classify Berkeley as a sceptic, and never write that he denies the external world.

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Berkeley establishes that minds and their ideas alone are real by five distinct arguments, only one of which is the notorious challenge about conceiving an unconceived object.
> **Named evidence.** *Principles* §§1–4 identify sensible things with collections of immediately perceived ideas; §8 denies that an idea can be like anything but an idea; §§9–15 extend Locke's own relativity arguments from secondary to primary qualities; §§22–23 challenge the reader to conceive an unconceived body; and §§6, 30–33 and 48 infer an infinite active spirit from the involuntary, law-governed order of sense.
> **Analysis.** The arguments are not redundant: the semantic argument fixes what a sensible thing is, the likeness principle removes the representative relation, the parity argument removes the last quality that might have belonged to matter, the Master Argument blocks the intuitive rejoinder, and the continuity argument supplies the positive metaphysics that replaces the deleted substratum.
> **Qualification.** Each rests on a contestable premise — the identification in §3 is a definition, relativity does not entail mind-dependence, and the Master Argument arguably confuses conceiving *that* an object is unconceived with conceiving *of* it as unconceived — so the system is best judged as internally economical rather than as demonstratively established.

#### Exam link

- **2021 Q1(c), 10 marks** — "Sensible things are those only which are immediately perceived by sense." Argument 1 is the direct answer; the word **immediately** is the hinge.
- **2018 Q3(a), 20 marks** — "How does Berkeley establish that Mind and its ideas alone are real?" is answered by naming all five arguments before turning to Moore and Russell.
- **2024 Q2(a), 20 marks** — arguments 2 and 3 are the "rejection of Locke's primary qualities" limb.

#### UPSC traps

| Trap | Correction |
|---|---|
| "Berkeley denies the external world" | He denies **matter**; the world of tables and chairs remains, as ideas sustained by God |
| Quoting *esse est percipi aut percipere* as Berkeley's Latin | The expansion is a later commentators' gloss; §3 gives only *esse est percipi* |
| Reducing the case to the Master Argument | It is the weakest of the five and the easiest to answer |
| Answering "immediately perceived" without contrasting Locke's mediated perception | The whole point of *immediately* is the denial of the veil |
| Calling him a sceptic | He is an **anti-sceptic** who blames scepticism on the materialist |

#### Revision notes

- Five arguments: semantic (§§1–4) · likeness (§8) · parity (§§9–15) · master (§§22–23) · continuity (§§6, 30–33, 48).
- *Esse est percipi* at §3, scoped to **sensible things**; spirits are known by notion.
- Deletes matter; keeps objects, science, other minds, God.
- Reality distinguished from imagination by involuntariness, vividness, coherence and law.
- Illusion concerns judgment, not the existence of the presented idea.
- Anti-sceptic: the materialist invents the unknowable world.

#### CLOSING RECALL FLOW — BERKELEY II: THE FIVE ARGUMENTS

```text
SENSIBLE THING = WHAT IS IMMEDIATELY PERCEIVED BY SENSE
        |
        v
FIVE ARGUMENTS FOR "MIND AND ITS IDEAS ALONE ARE REAL"
   1 SEMANTIC (Sec.1-4): objects = collections of ideas -> esse est percipi
   2 LIKENESS (Sec.8): an idea can be like only an idea
   3 PARITY (Sec.9-15): relativity hits primary qualities too
   4 MASTER (Sec.22-23): you cannot conceive the unconceived
   5 CONTINUITY (Sec.6, 30-33, 48): involuntary order -> infinite active spirit
        |
        +--> PRESUPPOSITIONS: definition | comparability | relativity=dependence |
        |    content of conceiving | only spirits act
        +--> OBJECTIONS: isomorphism rescues Locke | relativity is what a real
             shape produces | scope/use-mention confusion (Russell, Prior, Gallois)
        |
        v
DELETES: material substratum   KEEPS: tables, science, other minds, God
REALITY vs IMAGINATION: involuntary, vivid, coherent, law-governed
ILLUSION: error is in JUDGMENT, not in the existence of the idea
        |
        v
ANTI-SCEPTIC: the materialist invented the unknowable world
TRAP: "denies the world" | percipere as Berkeley's Latin | master-argument-only
ANSWER LINE: Berkeley deletes the substratum, never the table
```

### SESSION 9 — BERKELEY III: SPIRITS, NOTIONS, GOD, CONTINUITY, AND THE MOORE, RUSSELL AND HEGEL COMPARISONS

#### DEFINITION / WHAT THIS IS CALLED

**Plain-language definition:** Having deleted matter, Berkeley must say what is left: active minds, which he calls spirits, and the ideas they perceive; we know minds not by having a picture of them but by a different kind of awareness he calls a notion, and the biggest mind of all — God — is what keeps the world running when nobody human is looking.

**Technical definition:** Berkeley's positive ontology consists of **spirits** — active, indivisible substances that perceive and will — and their **ideas**, which are passive and inert; spirits are known by **notion** rather than by idea (*Principles* §§27, 89, 140), and the infinite spirit, **God**, causes the involuntary order of sense and secures the continued existence of sensible things unperceived by finite minds.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING OPENING — WRITE/ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Berkeley's God is not a piece of piety appended to an epistemology but the load-bearing member of it: having argued that ideas are passive and that only spirits act, he must explain why the ideas of sense arrive involuntarily and in a settled order, and the infinite spirit that supplies both the cause and the continuity is doing exactly the work Locke's matter had done — which is why the strongest criticism of immaterialism is not that it is impious but that it replaces an unknowable **substance** with an unperceivable **spirit**.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

- **spirit** — active, perceiving, willing; the only substance Berkeley retains
- **notion** — the non-idea awareness by which spirit is known
- **passivity of ideas** — the premise that forces an external cause
- **language of nature** — laws are the settled grammar of signs, not causes
- **continuity** — unperceived things persist because God perceives them
- **subjective idealism** versus **absolute idealism**
- **act–object distinction** — Moore's line of attack
- **logical construction** — Russell's alternative

**How to use them:** derive God from the passivity of ideas rather than asserting him; then take the two comparisons the papers actually demand — Moore and Russell in 2018, Hegel in 2024 — and treat them as matched axes, not as separate essays.

#### Plain-language visual — why Berkeley cannot stop at "ideas"

```text
   STEP 1  Look at what you perceive right now. You did not choose it.
           You cannot make the window vanish by willing it.
                             |
   STEP 2  Ideas are PASSIVE - a colour does not do anything.
           So an idea cannot be the cause of another idea.
                             |
   STEP 3  Something ACTIVE must be producing them.
           Not me: I did not choose them, and I cannot stop them.
                             |
   STEP 4  Only SPIRITS are active. So an OTHER spirit is producing them,
           one whose power and wisdom exceed mine.
                             |
   STEP 5  That spirit is GOD - and because he always perceives, the tree
           in the quad does not blink out when you leave the quadrangle.

   NOTE THE COST: Berkeley now knows something he cannot have an idea of.
   He answers: spirit is known by NOTION, not by idea, because an idea is
   passive and could never picture an activity. Critics call this ad hoc.
```

**In simple words:** Berkeley's argument for God is an argument from **involuntariness plus order**. It is not "somebody must be watching or things would vanish" as a nervous afterthought; it is the only available cause once ideas are declared inert. Present it as a derivation and the answer looks structural rather than devotional.

| Remember first | Meaning |
|---|---|
| Ideas | passive, inert, cannot cause |
| Spirits | active; perceive and will; not perceived as ideas |
| Notion | the awareness we have of spirit, of relations and of activity |
| God | the infinite spirit; cause of sense-ideas and guarantor of continuity |
| Laws of nature | the settled grammar of a divine language; **signs**, not causes |
| Other minds | inferred by analogy from purposive signs |

> **One-line memory:** ideas are **inert**, so the order of nature needs an **agent** — and that agent, not matter, is what sustains the unperceived.

#### 🖼️ VISUAL — the ontology, the two comparisons and the exact contrasts

```text
              | GOD  infinite spirit |--produces-->| ideas of SENSE |
              +----------------------+             | involuntary,   |

                        | sustains continuity      | law-governed   |
                        v                          +----------------+
              +----------------------+             +----------------+
              | FINITE SPIRITS       |--produces-->| ideas of       |
              | known: my own by     |             | IMAGINATION    |

              | others by ANALOGY    |             | faint,irregular|

   =====================================================================

   | AXIS              | MOORE                      | RUSSELL            |
   +-------------------+----------------------------+--------------------+

   +-------------------+----------------------------+--------------------+
```
